Bi-intuitionistic logics through the abstract algebraic logic lens
Jonte Deakin

Abstract
Since the discovery of critical mistakes in Rauszer’s work on bi-intuitionistic logics, solid foundations for these have progressively been rebuilt. However, the algebraic treatment of these logics has not yet been tended to. We fill this gap by algebraically analysing the bi-intuitionistic logics 𝗐𝖡𝖨𝖫 \mathsf{wBIL} and 𝗌𝖡𝖨𝖫 \mathsf{sBIL} . Given that these logics are only distinguished as consequence relations, and not as sets of theorems (hence the conflation in Rauszer’s work), the algebraic tools we use are tailored to the treatment of such relations. We mainly inspect these logics through the lens of abstract algebraic logic, but we also provide an alternative algebraic analysis of 𝗐𝖡𝖨𝖫 \mathsf{wBIL} and 𝗌𝖡𝖨𝖫 \mathsf{sBIL} as logic preserving degrees of truth and truth, respectively. Our results pertaining to 𝗐𝖡𝖨𝖫 \mathsf{wBIL} and 𝗌𝖡𝖨𝖫 \mathsf{sBIL} are formalised in the interactive theorem prover Rocq. Keywords: abstract algebraic logic, bi-intuitionistic logic, interactive theorem prover Rocq, algebraizability



1

, dual to intuitionistic implication
\to
.
This extension is mathematically natural, as with
\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

a symmetry is regained in the language
given that each operator now has a dual:

\top
has
\bot
,
\land
has
\lor
,
\to
has
\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

and
even
\neg\varphi:=\varphi\to\bot
has
{\sim}\varphi:=\top\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope

.
Despite being simple, this addition is surprisingly impactful as key constructive properties of intuitionistic
logic, like the disjunction property, are not retained in bi-intuitionistic logic.
This failure is notably due to the holding of a bi-intuitionistic version of the

law of excluded middle
:
\varphi\lor{\sim}\varphi
.
Still, the logic obtained
conservatively
extends intuitionistic logic, which implies
that the addition of the exclusion operator does not yet cause a collapse to classical logic.

Historically, bi-intuitionistic logic was developed by Cecylia Rauszer in a series of articles
in the 1970s
[
47
,
46
,
48
,
49
]
leading to her Ph.D. thesis
[
50
]
.
While appearances of bi-intuitionistic can be detected prior to these articles,
notably in 1942 in Moisil’s work
[
35
]
and in 1971 in Klemke’s work
[
28
]
,
the breadth and foundational nature of Rauszer’s work advocate for her place
as founder of this logic.
In particular, she studied this logic under a wide variety of aspects:
algebraic semantics, axiomatic calculus, sequent calculus, and Kripke semantics.
Unfortunately, through time a variety of mistakes have been detected in her work.
First, Crolard proved in 2001 that bi-intuitionistic logic is not complete for
the class of rooted frames
[
10
, Corollary 2.18]
.
However, the canonical model constructions of Rauszer are rooted, hence a contradiction.
Second, Pinto and Uustalu found in 2009
[
39
]
a counterexample to
Rauszer’s claim of admissibility of cut for the sequent calculus she designed
[
46
, Theorem 2.4]
.
Finally, Goré and Shillito in 2020
[
22
]
detected confusions in Rauszer’s work about the holding of the deduction theorem in bi-intuitionistic logic, as well as issues in her completeness proofs.

At the root of the issues in Rauszer’s work is a conflation: two
distinct
logics,
understood as consequence relations, were identified in her treatment of bi-intuitionistic logic
[
22
]
.
More precisely, Goré and Shillito exhibited a natural split between a
weak
and

strong
bi-intuitionistic logic,
repesctively called
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathsf{sBIL}
.
They showed that
(1)
\mathsf{sBIL}
is an extension of
\mathsf{wBIL}
,
(2)
\mathsf{wBIL}
satisfies the traditional deduction theorem while
\mathsf{sBIL}
only satisfies a modified version of it,
and that (3)
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathsf{sBIL}
respectively captured the
local
and
global
consequence
relations of the Kripke semantics for the bi-intuitionistic language.
This situation perfectly mirrors a known splitting phenomenon in modal logic,
where the
local
and the
global
modal logics satisfy (1), (2) and (3)
in the same way
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathsf{sBIL}
do.

Once this conflation is noted, the foundations laid by Rauszer can be disentangled and rectified.
This is effectively what was performed by Goré and Shillito
[
22
]
for the axiomatic calculi and Kripke semantics of
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathsf{sBIL}
.
This work was fully formalised in the interactive theorem prover Rocq (formerly Coq
[
55
]
)
in the second author’s PhD thesis
[
51
]
.
Similar results connecting the local
first-order
bi-intuitionistic logic and a Kripke semantics
with constant domains were formalised by Kirst and Shillito
[
27
]
.
Further, a constructive reverse analysis of the results of soundness and completeness for
\mathsf{wBIL}

with respect to the Kripke semantics was led in Rocq by Shillito and Kirst
[
52
]
.
Additionally, prior works on proof systems for bi-intuitionistic logic
[
23
,
10
,
58
,
39
,
56
,
30
,
24
,
6
]

can be assessed through the distinction between the two logics as pertaining to the local logic
\mathsf{wBIL}
.
In the first-order case, a recent work studied the local bi-intuitionistic logic
via polytree labelled sequent calculi
[
34
]
.
To date, a major pillar has been omitted in this revision process: the study of logic via algebras.
In this paper, we fill this gap by separating and studying

\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathsf{sBIL}
through algebraic semantics.

Given that
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathsf{sBIL}
can only be distinguished as consequence relations,
and not sets of theorems, we need algebraic tools tailored to such relations.
Note that traditional tools of algebraic semantics may not be helpful,
as they tend to define logics as sets of theorems and most often as the set of
formulas always interpreted as the top element of the algebras of a given class.

Abstract algebraic logic
, hereafter called AAL, is an approach to algebraic semantics
founded on the treatment of logics as consequence relations.
This field has developed over the last half century, with early
works by Rasiowa
[
44
]
and Czelakowski
[
12
]
.
Arguably, the start of what we know today as AAL begins with a 1986 work by Blok and Pigozzi
[
3
]
,
which they further developed in following years
[
2
,
5
]
.
From there on, this field was expanded by many and flourished into
modern abstract algebraic
logic
[
37
,
8
,
9
,
15
,
31
,
43
]
.
1
1

1

A place where one can find a much more detailed
history of abstract algebraic logic is the introduction to Font’s book on the
topic
[
14
]
.

With the tools of abstract algebraic logic, ties between logics and algebras
can be categorised according to their strength, giving rise to the so-called
Leibniz hierarchy
.
High enough in the hierarchy, some ties are so strong that they form
bridges

between a logic and a class of algebras, as they allow one to transfer properties of
the class of algebras into properties of the logic and vice-versa
[
2
,
11
,
29
]
.

In what follows we situate the logics
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathsf{sBIL}
in the Leibniz hierarchy.
We show that
\mathsf{sBIL}
stands high in this hierarchy, as it is
implicative and finitely algebraizable with respect to the class of bi-Heyting algebras.
The algebraizability of
\mathsf{sBIL}
allows us to use bridge theorems providing insights on
properties of
\mathsf{sBIL}
and of bi-Heyting algebras.
In comparison,
\mathsf{wBIL}
is located much lower in the hierarchy as it is only equivalential,
and not even finitely so.
The contrast between
\mathsf{sBIL}
and
\mathsf{wBIL}
is stark, as the former is tightly connected
to bi-Heyting algebras while we prove the latter to have no algebraic semantics (in the sense of AAL)
over these algebras.

While striking, the distinction between the two logics is clearly visible in the algebraic setting
only thanks to the general framework of AAL,
which crucially treats logics as consequence relations.
Such tools, developed in the late 1980s, were not available to Rauszer at the time
of her work on bi-intuitionistic logic in the 1970s.
So, as a by-product of our results, we would like to offer the following historical analysis
of the confusions in Rauszer’s works:
they are due to the absence, at the time, of a well-developed adequate framework to
study bi-intuitionistic logics as consequence relations.

To stay clear from the turmoils of the past,
we continue the line of work of the second author and formalise our results in Rocq,
using the full strength of classical logic in the metalogic.
Unfortunately, many results we rely on come from AAL, which is
an extremely abstract and general field.
Formalising this field would take years of community effort,
a task we have not tried to achieve in this paper.
Instead, we assume general AAL results to hold and formalise
results which pertain in particular to the logics
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathsf{sBIL}

and to the class of bi-Heyting algebras.
For clarity, each formalised results is flagged by a clickable Rocq logo

leading to its formalisation in Rocq.
Our entire formalisation can be conveniently explored on a web browser
(
https://ianshil.github.io/algbiint/toc.html
)
or downloaded
(
https://github.com/ianshil/algbiint
).

We start in Section
2
by introducing the elements of AAL necessary
to our work.
More precisely, we provide notions of universal algebra and discuss the Leibniz hierarchy.
Then, we lay in Section
3
the syntactic, axiomatic and Kripke semantic
foundations of bi-intuitionistic logics.
Section
4
is the core of our paper and is devoted to the situation of
the logics
\mathsf{sBIL}
and
\mathsf{wBIL}
, with the former treated first and the latter second.
We leverage this analysis in the Leibniz hierarchy in Section
5

by using bridge theorems to harvest results about
\mathsf{sBIL}
.
Before concluding in Section
7
, we make a step aside AAL in
Section
6
and
provide an alternative algebraic analysis of both logics through the notions
of logic
preserving truth
and
preserving degrees of truth
.

2
Abstract algebraic logic

In this section we recall the basic definitions and theorems from AAL which we
use in the sequel.
We do not aim to provide a complete introduction or explanation of AAL:
for this purpose, we refer to the textbook by Font
[
14
]
and Moraschini’s
monograph
[
37
]
.

2.1
Universal Algebra

AAL uses universal algebra for the algebraic part of algebraic logic.
So, we introduce the elements of universal algebra needed for our study.
2
2

2

The textbook by Burris and Sankappanavar
[
7
]
is a
good reference for an introduction to universal algebra.

Definition 2.1
.

A
language
is a pair
\mathbf{L}=(Op,ar)
where

Op
is a set of operator symbols, and

ar:Op\to\mathbb{N}
is a function assigning an arity to each operator symbol.

Definition 2.2
.

An
algebra

A
of type
\mathbf{L}
is a pair
(X,\{\lambda^{A}\mid\lambda\in Op\})
such that

X
is a set, and for all
\lambda
,
\lambda^{A}
is a function
\lambda^{A}:A^{n}\to A

for
n=ar(\lambda)
.

We often abuse notation and write
a\in A
, and
B\subseteq A
and
f:Y\to A
to mean

a\in X
, and
B\subseteq X
and
f:Y\to A
for an algebra
A=(X,\{\lambda^{A}\mid\lambda\in Op\})
.

Next, we define congruences, which are well behaved equivalence
relations used to define quotient algebras.

Definition 2.3
.

A
congruence relation
on an algebra
A
of type
\mathbf{L}

is a relation
\approx
satisfying the following.

(Refl)

a\approx a

(Symm)

If
a\approx b
then
b\approx a

(Trans)

If
a\approx b
and
b\approx c
then
a\approx c

(Cong)

If
a_{1}\approx b_{1}
and … and
a_{n}\approx b_{n}
, then for any
\lambda
with arity
n
,

\lambda^{A}a_{1}...a_{n}\approx\lambda^{A}b_{1}...b_{n}

The set of congruences on an algebra
A
will be written
Con(A)
.

The following definitions pertaining to congruences will help us situate the logic
\mathsf{wBIL}
in the Leibniz hierarchy.

Definition 2.4
.

Given a congruence relation
\approx
and an algebra
A
, we define the
quotient algebra

A/\!\!\approx
,
as having the underlying set as the quotient of the set by
\approx
and the operators

\lambda^{A/\approx}([a_{1}],...,[a_{n}])=[\lambda^{A}a_{1}...a_{n}]
3
3

3

This is a well defined algebra by the (Cong) property of
the congruence relation.

Given a class of algebras
\mathcal{K}
and an arbitrary algebra
A
, let

Con_{\mathcal{K}}(A)=\{\approx\ \in Con(A)\mid A/\!\!\approx\,\in\mathcal{K}\}

be the
relative congruences
of
A
with respect to
\mathcal{K}
.

We now draw our attention to formulas generated from a language.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\Prop
be a unique countably infinite set of propositional variables,
and
\mathbf{L}=(Op,ar)
a language.
The set
Form_{\mathbf{L}}
of formulas of
\mathbf{L}
is inductively defined through the following
clauses:

1.

if
p\in\Prop
then
p\in Form_{\mathbf{L}}
;

2.

for all
\lambda\in Op
with
ar(\lambda)=n
,
if
a_{i}\in Form_{\mathbf{L}}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
, then
\lambda a_{1}...a_{n}\in Form_{\mathbf{L}}
.

When
\lambda
is a binary connective we usually use infix notation,
i.e. we write
a_{1}\lambda a_{2}
for
\lambda a_{1}a_{2}
.
We omit the subscript of
Form_{\mathbf{L}}
when
\mathbf{L}
is clear from context.
We use the letters
p,q,r
to denote elements of
\Prop
, and
\varphi,\psi,\chi,\theta,\delta,\epsilon,\gamma,\eta,\zeta
to
denote elements of
Form_{\mathbf{L}}
, and
\Gamma,\Delta
for sets of formulas.
We also write
\overline{p}
for a sequence of proposition letters.
Often, a formula
\varphi
is written as
\varphi(\overline{p})
to indicate that
the proposition letters occurring in it are among those in the sequence

\overline{p}
.
We also use this convention for sets of formulas and write
\Gamma(\overline{p})

for
\Gamma
.

From now on, we distinguish algebraic and logical operators by adding a dot
on top of the algebraic ones, e.g.
\dot{\lambda}
.

We now define an interpretation of formulas into algebras.
4
4

4

Instead of the definition we give below, one can instead
use homomorphisms from the formula algebra of a language
\mathbf{L}

to interpret formulas in algebras of type
\mathbf{L}
.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
A
be an algebra of type
\mathbf{L}=(Op,ar)
.
A
valuation
on
A
is a function
v:\Prop\to A
.
Given a valuation
v
over
A
and a formula
\varphi\in Form_{\mathbf{L}}
,
we recursively define the
interpretation

\hat{v}(\varphi)
of
\varphi

in
A
via
v
as follows
5
5

5

As
Form_{\mathbf{L}}
is the free algebra in

\mathbf{L}
over
Prop
, this is equivalent to giving a homomorphism from

Form_{\mathbf{L}}
to
A
.
:

\hat{v}(p)

:=

v(p)

\hat{v}(\lambda\varphi_{1}\dots\varphi_{n})

:=

\dot{\lambda}(\hat{v}(\varphi_{1}))\dots(\hat{v}(\varphi_{n}))

We turn to the definition of equational consequence, which forms the semantic side of
abstract algebraic logic.

Definition 2.7
.

An
equation
is a pair of two formulas, usually written
\varphi=\psi
.

For a class of algebras
\mathcal{K}
, we define the
equational consequence
on
\mathcal{K}

as follows:

\Theta\vDash_{K}\varphi=\psi

if

\forall A\in\mathcal{K}.\,\forall v.\;\;[\forall\theta.\forall\eta.\;(\theta=\eta)\in\Theta\;\Rightarrow\;\hat{v}(\theta)=\hat{v}(\eta)]\;\Rightarrow\;\hat{v}(\varphi)=\hat{v}(\psi)

Where
\Theta
is a set of equations.

An algebra
A

validates

\varphi=\psi
if
\vDash_{\{A\}}\varphi=\psi
,
which we also write as
A\vDash\varphi=\psi
.
Similarly, a class of algebras
\mathcal{K}
validates
\varphi=\psi

if
\vDash_{\mathcal{K}}\varphi=\psi
which we also write
\mathcal{K}\vDash\varphi=\psi
.

A central notion in universal algebra is that of an algebraic variety.
While this concept only plays a minor role in our work,
we still make use of it.
6
6

6

Quasi-varieties also play an important role in AAL and
universal algebra, but we choose not to introduce this notion here as the only
algebraic class we treat in detail are bi-Heyting algebras, which are a
variety. A few of the theorems of AAL have been accordingly (but slightly) rewritten to
only apply to varieties.

Definition 2.8
.

A class of algebras
\mathcal{K}
is a
variety
if there is a set of equations
\Theta

such that
\mathcal{K}=\{A\mid A\vDash\Theta\}
.

As groups, rings, Heyting algebras and Boolean algebras can be defined
equationally, they are varieties.

2.2
Logics in the Leibniz hierarchy

Here, we define types of logics which appear in the Leibniz hierarchy.
To do so, we first need to define in the abstract what a logic is.
The notion we use originates with Tarski
[
53
]
,
where he considered logics as consequence relations.
His definition was in turn extended with substitution invariance
by Łoś and Suzko
[
32
]
.
This understanding of logic as consequence relation
is a cornerstone of AAL.

Definition 2.9
.

A
logic

\mathsf{L}
of type
\mathbf{L}
, is a relation, usually written
\vdash_{\mathsf{L}}
,
from
\mathcal{P}(Form_{\mathbf{L}})
to
Form_{\mathbf{L}}
such that the following hold.

(I)

If
\varphi\in\Gamma
, then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{L}}\varphi
;

(M)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{L}}\varphi
and
\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
, then
\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{L}}\varphi
;

(C)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{L}}\varphi
and
\Delta\vdash\psi
for every
\psi\in\Gamma
, then

\Delta\vdash_{\mathsf{L}}\varphi
;

(S)

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{L}}\varphi
, then
\sigma\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{L}}\sigma\varphi

for every substitution
\sigma
.

The conditions I,M,C,S are also referred to as Identity, Monotonicity, Cut and
Structurality, respectively. When the context is clear, we drop the subscript in
\vdash_{\mathsf{L}}
.

We make use of the standard shorthands, writing

\vdash\varphi

for

\emptyset\vdash\varphi

\Gamma,\psi\vdash\varphi

for

\Gamma\cup\{\psi\}\vdash\varphi

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\varphi

for

\Gamma\cup\Delta\vdash\varphi

\Gamma\vdash\Delta

for

\Gamma\vdash\delta\text{ for all }\delta\in\Delta

\Gamma\dashv\vdash\Delta

for

\Gamma\vdash\Delta\text{ and }\Delta\vdash\Gamma.

A
theory
is a set of formulas closed under consequence,
that is a set
\Gamma
such that
\Gamma\vdash\varphi
implies
\varphi\in\Gamma
.
For any logic we denote its set of theories as
Th_{\vdash}
.

A logic is
finitary
if, given
\Gamma\vdash\varphi
, then there exists a finite

\Delta\subseteq\Gamma
such that
\Delta\vdash\varphi
.

Next, relying on Definition
2.7
, we define what an algebraic semantics is
from the perspective of abstract algebraic logic.

Definition 2.10
.

For a logic
\mathsf{L}
, a class of algebras
\mathcal{K}
and a set of equations in one
variable
\tau(x)
,
\mathsf{L}
is said to have a
\tau(x)
-algebraic
semantics
if for all
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}\subseteq Form
,

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\quad\text{iff}\quad\tau(\Gamma)\vDash_{\mathcal{K}}\tau(\varphi)

where
\tau(\varphi)
is the set of equations
\tau(x)
where all occurrences of
x

are replaced by
\varphi
, and
\tau(\Gamma)=\bigcup\{\tau(\gamma)\mid\gamma\in\Gamma\}
.
We sometimes omit the mention of
x
in
\tau(x)
and allow ourselves to write
\tau
.

The notion of
\tau(x)
-algebraic semantics is not yet strong enough to connect
algebras and logics.
Indeed, many logics have strange and non-standard algebraic semantics.
For instance, following Glivenko’s theorem
[
21
]
, Heyting algebras
are a
\{\neg\neg x=\top\}
-algebraic semantics for classical logic.
Therefore, we need a way of ignoring pathological examples like this.
To do so, we introduce the notion of algebraizability.
The following definition was first given by Blok and Pigozzi
[
2
]
, with a
textbook account given by Font
[
14
, Definition 3.11]
.

Definition 2.11
.

A logic
\mathsf{L}
is
algebraizable with respect to a class
of algebras
\mathcal{K}
if there exists
a set of equations
\tau(x)
, called
defining equations
,
and formulas
\Delta(x,y)
, called
equivalence formulas
, such that
for all
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}\subseteq Form

and all sets of equations
\Theta\cup\{\epsilon=\delta\}
, the
following conditions are satisfied.

(ALG1)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi\quad\text{iff}\quad\tau(\Gamma)\vDash_{\mathcal{K}}\tau(\varphi)

(ALG2)

\Theta\vDash_{\mathcal{K}}\epsilon=\delta\quad\text{iff}\quad\Delta(\Theta)\vdash\Delta(\epsilon,\delta)

(ALG3)

\varphi\dashv\vdash\Delta(\tau(\varphi))

(ALG4)

\epsilon=\delta\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}\vDash_{\mathcal{K}}\tau(\Delta(\epsilon,\delta))

Note that we write
\Delta(\epsilon,\delta)
to mean the set of formulas
\Delta(x,y)

where all occurrences of
x
and
y
are respectively replaced by
\epsilon
and
\delta
,
and
\Delta(\Theta)=\bigcup\{\Delta(\epsilon,\delta)\mid(\epsilon=\delta)\in\Theta\}
for a set of equations
\Theta
.

We also say that
\mathsf{L}
is
algebraizable
if there exists a
class of algebras
\mathcal{K}
such that
\mathsf{L}
is algebraizable
with respect to that class.
If the defining equations and the equivalence formulas are finite, we say that

\mathsf{L}
is
finitely algebraizable
.

A logic can be algebraizable with respect to several classes of algebras.
However, there is a largest such class.

Definition 2.12
.

For any algebraizable logic
\mathsf{L}
, the
equivalent algebraic semantics

of
\mathsf{L}
is the largest class of algebras such that
\mathsf{L}
is algebraizable
with respect to that class.

The existence of an equivalent algebraic semantics for every algebraizable logic
[
14
, Corollary 3.18]

ensures that the above is well-defined.

Example 2.13
.

Boolean algebras are the equivalent algebraic semantics of classical logic,
Heyting algebras are the equivalent algebraic semantics of intuitionistic logic, and
modal algebras are the equivalent algebraic semantics of the global modal logic
\mathsf{K}
[
14
, Page 120]
.

Font
[
14
, Corollary 3.18]
gives an easy way to check if a variety is
the equivalent algebraic semantics of a logic.

Theorem 2.14
.

If
\mathsf{L}
is algebraizable with respect to a variety
\mathcal{V}
,
then
\mathcal{V}
is the equivalent algebraic semantics of
\mathsf{L}
.

Let us shift our focus to syntactic considerations on classes of logics in the
AAL setting.
The following definition is a syntactic characterisation of what it means for a
logic to be algebraizable
[
14
, Section 3.3]
.

Theorem 2.15
.

A logic
\mathsf{L}
is
algebraizable
if and only if there exist
a set of defining equations
\tau(x)
and equivalence formulas
\Delta(x,y)

such that the following conditions, together with (ALG3) from Definition
2.11
, hold.

(R)

\vdash\Delta(x,x)

(Sym)

\Delta(x,y)\vdash\Delta(y,x)

(Trans)

\Delta(x,y),\Delta(y,z)\vdash\Delta(x,z)

(Re)

\Delta(x_{1},y_{1}),...\Delta(x_{n},y_{n})\vdash\Delta(\lambda x_{1}...x_{n},\lambda y_{1}...y_{n})

for any
\lambda\in Op
with
ar(\lambda)=n

The first three condition tell us that
\Delta(x,y)
forms an equivalence relation,
while (Re) specifies it to a congruence.
Finally, (ALG3) ensures that the defining equations and equivalence formulas
do not lose information over the translation from logic to algebra and back to logic.

Most standard logics (intuitionistic, classical and global modal) are
algebraizable with equivalence formulas
\{x\to y,y\to x\}
and defining
equation
x=\top
.
Rasiowa and Sikorski
[
45
]
identified this pattern before the creation of AAL,
and abstracted the properties of implication necessary to be
algebraizable in the following way.

Definition 2.16
.

An
implicative logic
is some logic
\mathsf{L}
over a type
\mathbf{L}

with a binary operator
\to
such that the following holds, for any
p,q,r\in\Prop
and

\lambda\in Op
with
ar(\lambda)=n
.

(IL1)

\vdash p\to p

(IL2)

p\to q,q\to r\vdash p\to r

(IL3)

\{p_{k}\to q_{k}\mid 1\leq k\leq n\}\cup\{q_{k}\to p_{k}\mid 1\leq k\leq n\}\vdash\lambda p_{1}...p_{n}\to\lambda q_{1}...q_{n}

(IL4)

p,p\to q\vdash q

(IL5)

p\vdash q\to p

Using the syntactic characterisation of algebraizability of Theorem
2.15
,
it can easily be shown that implicative logics are algebraizable
[
14
, Proposition 3.15]
.
Therefore, implicative logics are sitting higher up in the Leibniz hierarchy.

Proposition 2.17
.

Implicative logics are finitely algebraizable with defining equation

\{x=x\to x\}
and equivalence formulas
\{x\to y,y\to x\}
.

Now, we use some of the properties of Theorem
2.15

to syntactically define significant classes of logics sitting below algebraizable
ones in the hierarchy
[
14
, Section 6.1]
.

Definition 2.18
.

A logic is called
protoalgebraic
if it has a set of equivalence formulas that
validate (R) and (MP’)
7
7

7

Usually this condition is called (MP), but we add the prime here
to distinguish it from the proof-theoretic rule (
MP
) given in Figure
1
.
,
shown below.

(MP’)

x,\Delta(x,y)\vdash y

An
equivalential logic
is a protoalgebraic whose
equivalence formulas also validate (Re).
For protoalgebraic and equivalential logics, the set
\Delta(x,y)

is also called the set of
congruence formulas
.
A finitely equivalential logic is an equivalential logic where
\Delta(x,y)
is finite.

To close this section, let us introduce the so-called “Isomorphism theorem”,
which presents an isomorphism between relative congruences
and
deductive filters
for algebraizable logics.
We start by defining deductive filters.

Definition 2.19
.

For any logic
\mathsf{L}
and algebra
A
, a
\mathsf{L}
-filter
(or
deductive filter
) is a set
F\subseteq A
such that, for all
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}\in Form
and valuation
v
over
A
:

\text{if }\Gamma\vdash\varphi\text{ and }\hat{v}(\Gamma)\subseteq F\text{ then }\hat{v}(\varphi)\in F.

We let
Fi_{\vdash}(A)
be the set of deductive filters on an algebra
A
.

Example 2.20
.

Classical logic-filters on Boolean algebras are precisely the lattice filters,
as are intuitionistic logic-filters on Heyting algebras.
Global modal logic
\mathsf{K}
-filters on modal algebras are precisely the open
filters i.e. the filters
F
such that if
p\in F
then
\square p\in F
[
14
, Exercise 2.31]
.

We now give the Isomorphism theorem, which was first proven by
Blok and Pigozzi
[
2
]
.

Theorem 2.21

(The Isomorphism Theorem)
.

If
\mathsf{L}
is algebraizable with equivalent algebraic semantics
\mathcal{K}
,
for any algebra
A
(not necessarily in
\mathcal{K}
), then there is a lattice
isomorphism between
Con_{\mathcal{K}}(A)
and
Fi_{\vdash}(A)
.

In our paper, we exploit this theorem to reduce a proof that a logic
is not algebraizable to a proof of the absence of such an isomorphism.

3
Basics of bi-intuitionistic logics

In this section we present the basics of the two bi-intuitionistic logics
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathsf{sBIL}
we are analysing
through the tools of AAL.
More precisely, we introduce their syntax, axiomatic proof systems, Kripke semantics
and known facts of relevance.
Most of what is presented here can be found in the work of Goré and Shillito
[
22
]

and in Shillito’s PhD thesis
[
51
]
.

3.1
Syntax

As mentioned above, bi-intuitionistic logics are expressed in the language of intuitionistic logic extended with the exclusion operator
\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

. More formally:

Definition 3.1

(
)
.

We define the propositional language
\mathbf{L}_{BI}=(Op_{BI},ar_{BI})
where
Op_{BI}=\{\top,\bot,\land,\lor,\to,\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope

and
ar_{BI}(\top)=ar_{BI}(\bot)=0
and
ar_{BI}(\land)=ar_{BI}(\lor)=ar_{BI}(\to)=ar_{BI}(\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope

. Alternatively, the formulas of
Form_{\mathbf{L}_{BI}}
can be seen as generated by the following grammar in Backus-Naur form:

\varphi::=p\in\Prop\mid\bot\mid\top\mid\varphi\,\land\,\varphi\mid\varphi\,\lor\,\varphi\mid\varphi\,\to\,\varphi\mid\varphi\,\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}\,\varphi

We define
\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi:=(\varphi\to\psi)\land(\psi\to\varphi)
.
We also define the abbreviations
\neg\varphi:=(\varphi\to\bot)
and
{\sim}\varphi:=(\top\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}\varphi)
, respectively called
negation
and
co-negation
(or also
weak
negation).

The added binary operator
\varphi\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}\psi
is intended to be the dual of

\varphi\to\psi
and is usually read as “
\varphi
excludes
\psi
”. Consequently,

{\sim}
is also the dual of
\neg
. As we often use repetitions of the pattern
\neg{\sim}
,
we recursively define
(\neg{\sim})^{n}\varphi
(
),
for a formula
\varphi
and a natural number
n
, to be
\varphi
if
n=0
,
and
\neg{\sim}(\neg{\sim})^{n-1}\varphi
otherwise.

For a formula
\varphi
, we recursively define its bi-depth
d(\varphi)
(
) on the structure of
\varphi
:
d(p)=d(\bot)=d(\bot)=1
for
p\in Prop
,
d(\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2})=d(\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2})=max(d(\varphi_{1}),d(\varphi_{2}))
, and
d(\varphi_{1}\to\varphi_{2})=d(\varphi_{1}\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}\varphi_{2})=max(d(\varphi_{1}),d(\varphi_{2}))+1
.

In the following, when
\Gamma
refers to a set of formulas, we write
\Gamma,\varphi
or
\varphi,\Gamma
to mean
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}
.

3.2
Axiomatic proof theory

Following AAL, we focus on logics as consequence relations and
axiomatically capture the two bi-intuitionistic logics using
generalised Hilbert calculi
,
which manipulate consecutions.

The generalised Hilbert calculi for
\mathsf{wBIL}
(
) and
\mathsf{sBIL}
(
)
[
22
]
, extend
the one for intuitionistic logic, containing the axioms
A_{1}
to
A_{10}
,
with the axioms
A_{11}
to
A_{14}
together with the rule (
wDN
) for
\mathsf{wBIL}

and the rule (
sDN
) for
\mathsf{sBIL}
, all shown in Figure
1
.
There,
\mathcal{A}
in the rule (
Ax
) refers to the set of all instances of axioms.

A derivation of
\Gamma\vdash\varphi
in
\mathsf{wBIL}
(resp.
\mathsf{sBIL}
) is a tree
of consecutions built using the rules (
MP
) and (
wDN
)
(resp. (
sDN
)) in Figure
1
with instances of (
Ax
)
and (
El
) as leaves.
If there is a derivation of
\Gamma\vdash\varphi
in
\mathsf{wBIL}
(resp.
\mathsf{sBIL}
),
we write
{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\varphi}
(resp.
{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi}
).
We write
{\Gamma}\not\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\varphi}
(resp.
{\Gamma}\not\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi}
)
if
{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\varphi}
(resp.
{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi}
) does not hold.
We also write
{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{i}}{\varphi}
to mean that
both

{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\varphi}
and
{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi}
,
and similarly for
{\Gamma}\not\vdash_{\mathsf{i}}{\varphi}
.
We formally define the logics
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathsf{sBIL}
as the sets

\{(\Gamma,\varphi):\;{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\varphi}\}
and

\{(\Gamma,\varphi):\;{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi}\}
respectively.

A_{1}

\varphi\to(\psi\to\varphi)

A_{2}

(\varphi\to(\psi\to\chi))\to((\varphi\to\psi)\to(\varphi\to\chi))

A_{3}

\varphi\to(\varphi\lor\psi)

A_{4}

\psi\to(\varphi\lor\psi)

A_{5}

(\varphi\to\chi)\to((\psi\to\chi)\to((\varphi\lor\psi)\to\chi))

A_{6}

(\varphi\land\psi)\to\varphi

A_{7}

(\varphi\land\psi)\to\psi

A_{8}

(\chi\to\varphi)\to((\chi\to\psi)\to(\chi\to(\varphi\land\psi)))

A_{9}

\bot\to\varphi

A_{10}

\varphi\to\top

Figure 1:
Generalised Hilbert calculus
\mathsf{wBIL}
for
\mathsf{wBIL}

Here, we present constructively-obtained proof-theoretic results from the work of Shillito
[
51
]
.
First, Shillito proves that both logics are
finitary logics
(
) (see Definition
2.9
).
Interesting properties of these logics, sometimes exclusive to one, are shown below.

Lemma 3.2
.

4.

{\Gamma,\varphi}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\psi}

\Leftrightarrow

{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\varphi\to\psi}

(\hrefhttps://ianshil.github.io/algbiint/GenHil.wBIH_meta_interactions.html#wBIH_Deduction_Theorem,\hrefhttps://ianshil.github.io/algbiint/GenHil.wBIH_meta_interactions.html#wBIH_Detachment_Theorem)

5.

{\Gamma,\varphi}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\psi}

\Leftrightarrow

\exists n\in\mathbb{N}.\;\;{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{(\neg{\sim})^{n}\varphi\to\psi}

(\hrefhttps://ianshil.github.io/algbiint/GenHil.sBIH_meta_interactions.html#gen_sBIH_Deduction_Theorem,\hrefhttps://ianshil.github.io/algbiint/GenHil.sBIH_meta_interactions.html#gen_sBIH_Double_Negated_Detachment_Theorem)

(1) above shows that a bi-intuitionistic version of the law of excluded-middle holds in both logics.
(2) is a syntactic analogue of the algebraic dual residuation property below.

\inferLineSkip=3pta\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}b\leq ca\leq b\lor c

This law holds in
\mathsf{sBIL}
even when the context of the consecution is not empty, as shown in (3).
While (4) is the deduction theorem for
\mathsf{wBIL}
,
it does not hold for
\mathsf{sBIL}
, which satisfies the modified version in (5).

3.3
Relational semantics

We proceed to define the traditional relational (Kripke) semantics for the bi-intuitionistic language
[
50
]
.
This semantics uses (Kripke) models identical to the ones of intuitionistic logic.

Definition 3.3

(
)
.

A model
\mathfrak{M}
is a tuple
(W,\leq,I)
,
where
(W,\leq)
is a preordered set and

I:\Prop\to\mathcal{P}(W)
is a
persistent
interpretation function
(where
\mathcal{P}(W)
is the powerset of
W
) i.e:

\forall v,w\in W.\,\forall p\in\Prop.\;(w\leq v\,\Rightarrow\,w\in I(p)\,\Rightarrow\,v\in I(p))

This semantics extends the forcing relation of intuitionistic logic to
\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}
in the following way.

Definition 3.4

(
)
.

Given a model
\mathfrak{M}=(W,\leq,I)
, we define the forcing relation
\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\varphi

between a world
w\in W
and a formula
\varphi
as follows, where
\mathfrak{M},w\not\Vdash\varphi

means that
\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\varphi
does not hold:

\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash p

:=

w\in I(p)

\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\top

:=

always

\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\bot

:=

never

\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\varphi\land\psi

:=

\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\varphi\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\psi

\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\varphi\lor\psi

:=

\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\varphi\text{ or }\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\psi

\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\varphi\to\psi

:=

\forall v\geq w.\;(\mathfrak{M},v\Vdash\varphi\text{ implies }\;\mathfrak{M},v\Vdash\psi)

\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\varphi\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}\psi

:=

\exists v\leq w.\;(\mathfrak{M},v\Vdash\varphi\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},v\not\Vdash\psi)

Crucially, while the semantic clause for
\to
looks
forward
on the
relation
\leq
, the clause for
\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}
looks
backward
.

8

Note that in our formalisation, we use the classically equivalent but intuitionistically
weaker interpretation of
\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}
given by Shillito and Kirst
[
52
]
:

(\forall v\leq w.(\mathfrak{M},v\Vdash\varphi\text{ implies }\mathfrak{M},v\Vdash\psi))
is false.

This causes bi-intuitionistic logic to share similarities with tense logic
[
40
,
41
,
42
]
.

As already mentioned, the
\neg{\sim}
pattern plays a role of importance in our work, as
it is crucial in the distinction between
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathsf{sBIL}
via the rules

(\text{wDN})
and
(\text{sDN})
.
Here, we would like to draw the attention of the reader to the semantic
meaning of this pattern.
To see it, we introduce the following notions.

Definition 3.5
.

Given a Kripke model
\mathfrak{M}
, and two elements
w,v\in W

we write
w\lessgtr v
if there exists a
u\in A
such that
w\leq u
and
v\leq u
.
We call the relation
\lessgtr
the
zig-zag
relation.
Additionally, we write
w\lessgtr^{n}v
if there exists

u_{1},...,u_{n-1}
such that
w\lessgtr u_{1}\lessgtr u_{2}\dots u_{n-1}\lessgtr v
,
with special case
w\lessgtr^{0}v
when
w=v
(
).
We say
w
and
v
are
connected
, written
v\bowtie w
,
if
w\lessgtr^{n}v
for some
n
(
).

One can view
\neg{\sim}
as a
\Box
modality over the relation
\lessgtr

[
51
, Lemma 8.9.2]
.

Lemma 3.6

(
)
.

For any Kripke model
\mathfrak{M}
and world
w
in
\mathfrak{M}
, the following holds.

\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash(\neg{\sim})^{n}\varphi

\Leftrightarrow

\forall v.\;w\lessgtr^{n}v\;\Rightarrow\;\mathfrak{M},v\Vdash\varphi

Proof.

First note that
\neg{\sim}\varphi
is just a shorthand for
(\top\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}\varphi)\to\bot
.
So,
\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash(\top\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}\varphi)\to\bot
entails that for all
u\geq w
we have
that
\mathfrak{M},u\Vdash(\top\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

implies
\mathfrak{M},u\Vdash\bot
.
Given that
\mathfrak{M},u\Vdash\bot
is a contradiction, we know that
\mathfrak{M},u\not\Vdash(\top\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}\varphi)

holds for all
u\geq w
.
This means that there cannot exist a world
v\leq u
such that
\mathfrak{M},v\not\Vdash\varphi
.
Hence if
\mathfrak{M},v\Vdash\neg{\sim}\varphi
, then
\mathfrak{M},v\Vdash\varphi
for any world
v
such that
v\lessgtr w
.

By our previous observation, and an induction on the natural numbers, we can conclude that

\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash(\neg{\sim})^{n}\varphi
if and only if for all
v\lessgtr^{n}w
we have
\mathfrak{M},v\Vdash\varphi
.
∎

We conclude our digression on
\neg{\sim}
by a way to visualise what
\neg{\sim}\varphi
means on a model:
it is as a sort of
jellyfish
.
The forcing of this formula in a world
w
entails the forcing of
\varphi

in the downset of all the elements in the upset of
w
.
In this light, the upset of
w
is the body of the jellyfish with the
downset of the upsets its tentacles.

An important feature of the relational semantics for intuitionistic logic is
persistence. The second author
[
51
, Lemma 8.7.1]
proves that persistence is
preserved in the semantics extended to
\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}
.

Lemma 3.7

(Persistence
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}=(W,\leq,I)
be a model. The following holds.

\forall v,w\in W.\,\forall\varphi.\,(w\leq v\,\Rightarrow\,(\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\varphi\,\Rightarrow\,\mathfrak{M},v\Vdash\varphi))

The models in our semantics can be more or less similar, depending on the notion of “similarity” we have in mind.
Such a notion, which we exploit in this paper, is
n
-bisimilarity
.
We take inspiration from Badia
[
1
, Definition 1]
and
Pattinson and de Groot
[
13
, Definition 4.1]
to define
n
-bisimulation;
although our definition is equivalent to neither.
9
9

9

To be precise, we take the idea to define

n
-bisimulations from Badia, and we take the
general form of bi-intuitionistic bisimulation from Pattinson
and de Groot.

Definition 3.8

(
n
-bisimilarity
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}=(W,\leq,I)
and
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}=(W^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},I^{\prime})
be models,
w
be a world in

W
and
w^{\prime}
be a world in
W^{\prime}
.
We define the property
\mathfrak{M},w\rightleftharpoons_{n}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},w^{\prime}
recursively on
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We have
\mathfrak{M},w\rightleftharpoons_{0}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},w^{\prime}
if the condition (B1) below is satisfied.

(B1)

\forall p\in Prop.\,(w\in I(p)\;\Leftrightarrow\;w^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}(p))

We have
\mathfrak{M},w\rightleftharpoons_{m+1}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},w^{\prime}
if the conditions (B1-B5), are satisfied.

(B2)

\forall v\in W.\,(

v\leq w

\Rightarrow

\exists v^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}.\,(v^{\prime}\leq^{\prime}w^{\prime}\;\text{ and }\;\mathfrak{M},v\rightleftharpoons_{m}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},v^{\prime})

)

(B3)

\forall v\in W.\,(

w\leq v

\Rightarrow

\exists v^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}.\,(w^{\prime}\leq^{\prime}v^{\prime}\;\text{ and }\;\mathfrak{M},v\rightleftharpoons_{m}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},v^{\prime})

)

(B4)

\forall v^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}.\,(

v^{\prime}\leq^{\prime}w^{\prime}

\Rightarrow

\exists v\in W.\,(v\leq w\;\text{ and }\;\mathfrak{M},v\rightleftharpoons_{m}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},v^{\prime})

)

(B5)

\forall v^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}.\,(

w^{\prime}\leq^{\prime}v^{\prime}

\Rightarrow

\exists v\in W.\,(w\leq v\;\text{ and }\;\mathfrak{M},v\rightleftharpoons_{m}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},v^{\prime})

)

If
\mathfrak{M},w\rightleftharpoons_{n}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},w^{\prime}
holds, we say that
w
and
w^{\prime}
are
n
-bisimilar
.

The point of this definition is that
when
w
and
w^{\prime}
are
n
-bisimilar they are indistinguishable by formulas of
bi-depth smaller or equal to
n
. We prove this as follows.

Lemma 3.9

(
)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathfrak{M}=(W,\leq,I)
and
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}=(W^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},I^{\prime})
be models.
For any
w\in W
and
w^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}
, such that
\mathfrak{M},w\rightleftharpoons_{n}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},w^{\prime}
we have the following:

\forall\varphi.\,(d(\varphi)\leq n\;\Rightarrow\;(\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\varphi\,\Leftrightarrow\,\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},w^{\prime}\Vdash\varphi)).

Proof.

The proof goes by primary induction on
n
and secondary induction on the structure of
\varphi
.
Note that the case where
n=0
is trivial, as there is no formula
\varphi
with
d(\varphi)\leq 0
.
So, consider the case where
n=m+1
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
We now inspect the structure of
\varphi
.
The base case goes through trivially thanks to conditions (B1).
We only consider the remaining cases of
\to
and
\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}
as the
others are straightforward.

\varphi=\psi\to\chi:
Assume that
\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\psi\to\chi
. We need to show that

\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},w^{\prime}\Vdash\psi\to\chi
. Let
v^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}
such that
w^{\prime}\leq^{\prime}v^{\prime}
. We assume that

\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},v^{\prime}\Vdash\psi
and aim to prove that
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},v^{\prime}\Vdash\psi
. Now, condition (B5) together
with both
\mathfrak{M},w\rightleftharpoons_{m+1}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},w^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime}\leq^{\prime}v^{\prime}
gives us
the existence of a
v\in W
such that
w\leq v
and
\mathfrak{M},v\rightleftharpoons_{m}\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},v^{\prime}
.
Thus, we can use the primary induction on
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},v^{\prime}\Vdash\psi
to get
\mathfrak{M},v\Vdash\psi
.
Therefore, we get
\mathfrak{M},v\Vdash\chi
as
w\leq v
and
\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\psi\to\chi
.
By primary induction again, we obtain
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},v^{\prime}\Vdash\chi
. As
v^{\prime}
is arbitrary, we get that

\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},w^{\prime}\Vdash\psi\to\chi
. The other direction is symmetric.

Soundness is proven as usual by showing that all axioms are valid and that rules of each logic preserve the corresponding semantic consequence.
Completeness for
\mathsf{wBIL}
is proven via a canonical model construction, which has prime theories as points
[
52
]
. The proof for
\mathsf{sBIL}

[
51
, Theorem 8.9.1]
relies on a transformation of the canonical model for
\mathsf{wBIL}
(
).

4
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathsf{sBIL}
in the Leibniz hierarchy

Relying on the semantic and proof-theoretic characterisations of
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathsf{sBIL}
given in the last section,
we can use the tools of Section
2
to situate these logics in the Leibniz hierarchy.
In this section we introduce bi-Heyting algebras, as the class
\mathcal{BHA}
of these algebras plays
a crucial role for both
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathsf{sBIL}
.
Then, we locate
\mathsf{sBIL}
in the hierarchy by showing that it is implicative and finitely algebraizable
with respect to
\mathcal{BHA}
.
Finally, we consider
\mathsf{wBIL}
and show that is not algebraizable but
instead equivalential, despite not being finitely so.
The disconnection between
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathcal{BHA}
in abstract algebraic logic is further
witnessed by the absence of algebraic semantics for the former on the latter.

4.1
Bi-Heyting algebras

We introduce bi-Heyting algebras as extensions of Heyting algebras, which themselves extend
bounded lattices. These notions are successively defined below.

Definition 4.1
.

A
bounded lattice
is an algebraic structure
(X,\dot{\top},\abot,\aland,\alor)
where
X

is a set with
\dot{\top}
and
\abot
as nullary operators, and
\aland
and
\alor
as binary
operators on
X
, satisfying the following equalities.

(P1)

a\aland\dot{\top}

=

a

(P2)

a\alor\abot

=

a

(P3)

a\aland b

=

b\aland a

(P4)

a\aland(b\aland c)

=

(a\aland b)\aland c

(P5)

a\aland(a\alor b)

=

a

(P6)

a\alor b

=

b\alor a

(P7)

a\alor(b\alor c)

=

(a\alor b)\alor c

(P8)

a\alor(a\aland b)

=

a

The binary relation
\leq
on a bounded lattice
L
, defined as
a\leq b
if
a=a\aland b
,
is a partial order (reflexive
, transitive
, antisymmetric
) with

\dot{\top}
and
\abot
as top (
) and bottom (
) elements.

While we use the order
\leq
given above to define more naturally (bi-)Heyting algebras,
note that this is just a notation: our algebras are ultimately defined algebraically (i.e. with
equations) and not order-theoretically (i.e. with
\leq
as primitive).

Definition 4.2
.

A
Heyting algebra
is a tuple
(X,\dot{\top},\abot,\aland,\alor,\ato)
such that

(X,\dot{\top},\abot,\aland,\alor)
is a bounded lattice and
\ato
is a binary operator
on
X
satisfying the following law.

(ResI)

a\aland b\leq c

\Leftrightarrow

a\leq b\ato c

A
bi-Heyting algebra
(
) is a tuple
(X,\dot{\top},\abot,\aland,\alor,\ato,\aexcl)

such that
(X,\dot{\top},\abot,\aland,\alor,\ato)
is a Heyting algebra and
\aexcl

is a binary operator on
X
satisfying the following law.

(ResE)

a\leq b\alor c

\Leftrightarrow

a\aexcl b\leq c

Bi-Heyting algebras inherit many properties of Heyting algebras, but enjoy
many more.
Some of these additional properties which are useful to our work are
shown below.

Lemma 4.3
.

For any bi-Heyting algebra
A
, and
a,b\in A
, the following hold.

1.

\awneg\awneg a\leq a

2.

a\leq b\alor(a\aexcl b)

3.

a\aexcl b\leq a\aland\awneg b

Proof.

Recall that
\awneg a
is an abbreviation for
\dot{\top}\aexcl a
. The needed proofs
are as follows:

1.

\dot{\top}\aexcl a

\leq

\dot{\top}\aexcl a

\dot{\top}

\leq

(\dot{\top}\aexcl a)\alor a

(ResE)

\dot{\top}\aexcl(\dot{\top}\aexcl a)

\leq

a

(ResE)

2.

a\aexcl b

\leq

a\aexcl b

a

\leq

b\alor(a\aexcl b)

(ResE)

3.

a

\leq

b\alor a

a

\leq

(b\alor a)\aland\dot{\top}

(boundedness of
A
)

a

\leq

(b\alor a)\aland(b\alor(\dot{\top}\aexcl b))

(Lemma
4.3
.2)

a

\leq

b\alor(a\aland(\dot{\top}\aexcl b))

(distributivity of
\alor
)

a\aexcl b

\leq

a\aland(\dot{\top}\aexcl b)

(ResE)

∎

4.2
\mathsf{sBIL}
is algebraizable

We begin our study on the AAL perspective on bi-intuitionistic logics with a look at
\mathsf{sBIL}
.
In particular we seek to show that
\mathsf{sBIL}
is algebraizable with
\mathcal{BHA}
as its equivalent algebraic semantics.
First, we proceed to show that it is (finitely) algebraizable by showing that it is implicative.
Second, we refine this statement and show in a direct way that
\mathcal{BHA}
is
the equivalent algebraic semantics of
\mathsf{sBIL}
.

Let us show that
\mathsf{sBIL}
is implicative in a purely proof-theoretic way.
As
\mathsf{sBIL}
is an extension of intuitionistic logic, the only difficulty resides

2.16
.

As a consequence of
\mathsf{sBIL}
being implicative and
Proposition
2.17
we have the following.

Corollary 4.5
.

\mathsf{sBIL}
is finitely algebraizable with defining equation
p=\top

and equivalence formulas
p\leftrightarrow q
.
10
10

10

For conciseness of notation we use the formula
p\leftrightarrow q

instead of
\{p\to q,q\to p\}
. This is legitimate here as in our logics we have

p\leftrightarrow q\dashv\vdash\{p\to q,q\to p\}
.

Next, we want to refine the statement above and exhibit a specific class of
algebras with respect to which
\mathsf{sBIL}
is algebraizable.
Concretely, we show that
\mathsf{sBIL}
is algebraizable with respect to
\mathcal{BHA}

with defining equation
p=\top
and equivalence formula
p\leftrightarrow q
.
To obtain this result, we need to show the holding of all four conditions of
Definition
2.11
.
We first prove (ALG3) and (ALG4) in a proof-theoretic
and algebraic way, respectively.

Proposition 4.6

(ALG3
)
.

The following holds.

{\varphi}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi\leftrightarrow\top}

{\varphi\leftrightarrow\top}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi}

Proof.

Consider the proofs below, and note that the two topmost ones establish
{\varphi}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi\leftrightarrow\top}
.

Lem.

\varphi\vdash\top

(
Ax
)

\varphi\vdash\top\to\varphi\to\top

(
MP
)

\varphi\vdash\varphi\to\top

(
El
)

\varphi\vdash\varphi

(
Ax
)

\varphi\vdash\varphi\to\top\to\varphi

(
MP
)

\varphi\vdash\top\to\varphi

Lem.

\varphi\leftrightarrow\top\vdash\top

(
El
)

\varphi\leftrightarrow\top\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\top

(
Ax
)

\varphi\leftrightarrow\top\vdash(\varphi\leftrightarrow\top)\to(\top\to\varphi)

(
MP
)

\varphi\leftrightarrow\top\vdash\top\to\varphi

(
MP
)

\varphi\leftrightarrow\top\vdash\varphi

∎

Proposition 4.7

(ALG4
)
.

The following holds.

\epsilon=\delta\vDash_{\mathcal{BHA}}(\epsilon\leftrightarrow\delta)=\top

(\epsilon\leftrightarrow\delta)=\top\vDash_{\mathcal{BHA}}\epsilon=\delta

Proof.

To prove
\epsilon=\delta\vDash_{\mathcal{BHA}}(\epsilon\leftrightarrow\delta)=\top
it suffices
to prove
\epsilon=\delta\vDash_{\mathcal{BHA}}(\epsilon\to\delta)=\top
and

\epsilon=\delta\vDash_{\mathcal{BHA}}(\delta\to\epsilon)=\top
.
We provide the proof of the former but omit the one of the latter, as it is symmetric.
Let
A\in\mathcal{BHA}
and
v
a valuation over
A
.
Assume that
\hat{v}(\epsilon)=\hat{v}(\delta)
.
We need to show
\hat{v}(\epsilon)\ato\hat{v}(\delta)=\dot{\top}
. Under our assumption it
suffices to show
\hat{v}(\epsilon)\ato\hat{v}(\epsilon)=\dot{\top}
, which is straightforwardly provable.

We are left to prove
(\epsilon\leftrightarrow\delta)=\top\vDash_{\mathcal{BHA}}\epsilon=\delta
.
Let
A\in\mathcal{BHA}
and
v
a valuation over
A
.
Assume that
\hat{v}(\epsilon)\aleftrightarrow\hat{v}(\delta)=\dot{\top}
.
By the antisymmetry of
\leq
,
\hat{v}(\epsilon)=\hat{v}(\delta)
can be proved by
showing
\hat{v}(\epsilon)\leq\hat{v}(\delta)
and
\hat{v}(\epsilon)\geq\hat{v}(\delta)
.
In turn, these are easily obtained via our assumption,
(ResI)
, (P1) and the properties of
\aland
.
∎

What remains to be shown is that conditions (ALG1) and (ALG2) hold. To prove
these we proceed with a traditional algebraic soundness and completeness result
relying on a Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra.

For soundness it suffices to show that the axioms and rules of
\mathsf{sBIL}

all hold on bi-Heyting algebras.

Proposition 4.8

(Soundness
)
.

If
{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi}
then
\{\gamma=\top\mid\gamma\in\Gamma\}\vDash_{\mathcal{BHA}}\varphi=\top
.

Proof.

We reason by induction on the structure of the proof of
\Gamma\vdash\varphi
.
The case for
(\text{El})
is straightforward.
The case for
(\text{Ax})
consists in showing that in any bi-Heyting algebra all
axioms are interpreted as
\dot{\top}
(
). Using the antisymmetry of
\leq

and the fact that
\dot{\top}
is a top element, it is sufficient to show
all axioms are greater than
\dot{\top}
. The proof for all intuitionistic axioms are
as usual (
,

,
,
).

Assume that
\hat{v}(\varphi)=\dot{\top}

\dot{\top}\aexcl\dot{\top}=\abot
,
\dot{\top}\aexcl\hat{v}(\varphi)=\abot

To prove completeness we use
Lindenbaum-Tarski algebras
, i.e. algebras whose
points are classes of formulas which are
\mathsf{i}
-equivalent modulo
\Gamma

for a given
\Gamma
.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
\{\varphi,\psi\}\cup\Gamma\subseteq Form
and
i\in\{w,s\}
.
We say that
\varphi
and
\psi

\mathsf{i}
-equivalent modulo
\Gamma
if

{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{i}}{\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi}
.
The set of formulas
\mathsf{i}
-equivalent modulo
\Gamma
to
\varphi
is denoted
by
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{i}}
(
).
The class of all sets
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{i}}
is denoted

\llbracket Form\rrbracket^{\mathsf{i}}_{\Gamma}
.
When
\mathsf{i}
and
\Gamma
are clear from context, we drop their mention and
talk about
equivalent
formulas and write
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
and
\llbracket Form\rrbracket^{\mathsf{}}
.

For the remaining of our discussion of the completeness proof let us
fix a set of formulas
\Gamma
and let us reiterate that
\mathsf{sBIL}
is the logic
we focus on.
Given that we want our Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra to a be bi-Heyting algebra, we need to
define an operator on equivalent classes for each and every algebraic operator
satisfying adequate properties.

Quite naturally, one would want to use logical connectives on representatives
of equivalence classes.
For example, we can define the algebraic operator
\aland

in our Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra as taking two equivalence classes
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
and

\llbracket\psi\rrbracket
, with respective representative
\varphi
and
\psi
, and giving
\llbracket\varphi\land\psi\rrbracket
.
Through this approach one needs to prove that the choice of representative does not
matter: by taking any
\varphi^{\prime}\in\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
and
\psi^{\prime}\in\llbracket\psi\rrbracket
, we must have

\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\land\psi^{\prime}\rrbracket=\llbracket\varphi\land\psi\rrbracket
.
While this result can be proved for all intuitionistic connectives in both
\mathsf{wBIL}
and

\mathsf{sBIL}
(
,
,
,
,
), interestingly we can
only prove this result for
\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Crucially, left monotonicity and right anti-monotonicity of

{\Gamma}\not\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{(p_{1}\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

,
).

We can therefore define our Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra as a bi-Heyting algebra.

Definition 4.10

(Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra)
.

We define
\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\Gamma}=(\llbracket Form\rrbracket^{\mathsf{s}}_{\Gamma},\dot{\top},\abot,\aland,\alor,\ato,\aexcl)

to be the bi-Heyting algebra with the operators defined below.

\dot{\top}:=\llbracket\top\rrbracket_{\Gamma}

\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\Gamma}\aland\,\llbracket\psi\rrbracket_{\Gamma}:=\llbracket\varphi\land\psi\rrbracket_{\Gamma}

\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\Gamma}\ato\,\llbracket\psi\rrbracket_{\Gamma}:=\llbracket\varphi\to\psi\rrbracket_{\Gamma}

\abot:=\llbracket\bot\rrbracket_{\Gamma}

\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\Gamma}\alor\,\llbracket\psi\rrbracket_{\Gamma}:=\llbracket\varphi\lor\psi\rrbracket_{\Gamma}

\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\Gamma}\aexcl\,\llbracket\psi\rrbracket_{\Gamma}:=\llbracket\varphi\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}\psi\rrbracket_{\Gamma}

A crucial property of the Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra
\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\Gamma}
is that
formulas are interpreted as their equivalence class under the valuation mapping
propositional variables to their equivalence class.

Lemma 4.11

(
)
.

Let
v_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{}}}:Prop\to\llbracket Form\rrbracket^{\mathsf{}}
be such that
v_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{}}}(p)=\llbracket p\rrbracket
. We have that
\hat{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{}}}(\varphi)=\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward:
it goes by induction on the structure of
\varphi
, and relies on
definitional equalities to go through, like in the following
\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
