{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}
case:

\hat{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{}}}(\psi\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

\lxSVG@closescope

.
∎

With this result in hand, we can easily prove completeness.

Proposition 4.12

(Completeness
)
.

If
\{\gamma=\top\mid\gamma\in\Gamma\}\vDash_{\mathcal{BHA}}\varphi=\top
then
{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi}
.

Proof.

We prove the contrapositive. So, assume
{\Gamma}\not\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi}
.
By Lemma
4.11
we get that
\hat{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{}}}(\varphi)=\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
and
\hat{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{}}}(\gamma)=\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket

for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
Clearly, we have for any such
\gamma
that
\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket=\llbracket\top\rrbracket=\dot{\top}
as
{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\gamma\leftrightarrow\top}
.
However, we also have that
{\Gamma}\not\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\top\rightarrow\varphi}
because of our assumption
{\Gamma}\not\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi}
.
Consequently, we must have that
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket\neq\llbracket\top\rrbracket
.
Therefore, we exhibited a bi-Heyting algebra and an interpretation on it witnessing

\{\gamma=\top\mid\gamma\in\Gamma\}\not\vDash_{\mathcal{BHA}}\varphi=\top

∎

As promised, using soundness and completeness for
\mathsf{sBIL}
we can straightforwardly
prove the remaining conditions of algebraizability (ALG1) and (ALG2).
In fact, (ALG1) is nothing but the combination of soundness and completeness.

Proposition 4.13

(ALG1
)
.

The following holds.

{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi}

if and only if

\{\gamma=\top\mid\gamma\in\Gamma\}\vDash_{\mathcal{BHA}}\varphi=\top

Proposition 4.14

(ALG2
)
.

The following holds.

\Theta\vDash_{\mathcal{BHA}}\epsilon=\delta

if and only if

{\{\theta_{1}\leftrightarrow\theta_{2}\mid(\theta_{1}=\theta_{2})\in\Theta\}}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\epsilon\leftrightarrow\delta}

Proof.

We start by proving the left-to-right direction and assume
\Theta\vDash_{\mathcal{BHA}}\epsilon=\delta
.
Using Proposition
4.12
we can reduce our goal to

\{(\theta_{1}\leftrightarrow\theta_{2})=\top\mid(\theta_{1}=\theta_{2})\in\Theta\}\vDash_{\mathcal{BHA}}(\epsilon\leftrightarrow\delta)=\top.

Through the antisymmetry of
\leq
, assuming that
\hat{v}(\theta_{1}\leftrightarrow\theta_{2})=\dot{\top}

is equivalent to assuming
\hat{v}(\theta_{1})=\hat{v}(\theta_{2})
.
Therefore, assuming all the equations
\{(\theta_{1}\leftrightarrow\theta_{2})=\top\mid(\theta_{1}=\theta_{2})\in\Theta\}

to hold amounts to assuming the set of equations
\Theta
.
Using our initial hypothesis we get that
\hat{v}(\epsilon)=\hat{v}(\delta)
.
It is then trivial to prove that the equation
(\epsilon\leftrightarrow\delta)=\top
holds.

We turn to the remaining direction and assume

{\{\theta_{1}\leftrightarrow\theta_{2}\mid(\theta_{1}=\theta_{2})\in\Theta\}}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\epsilon\leftrightarrow\delta}
.
By applying Proposition
4.8
to this assumption we obtain

\{(\theta_{1}\leftrightarrow\theta_{2})=\top\mid(\theta_{1}=\theta_{2})\in\Theta\}\vDash_{\mathcal{BHA}}(\epsilon\leftrightarrow\delta)=\top.

Here again, we prove
\Theta\vDash_{\mathcal{BHA}}\epsilon=\delta
using the
equivalence between the equations
\varphi=\psi
and
\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi=\top
.
∎

Now that we have proved that all conditions hold for the class
\mathcal{BHA}
with defining equation

p=\top
and equivalence formula
p\leftrightarrow q
, we obtain our precise algebraizability result.

Theorem 4.15
.

\mathsf{sBIL}
is (finitely) algebraizable with respect to
\mathcal{BHA}
with defining equations

\{p=\top\}
and equivalence formulas
\{p\leftrightarrow q\}
.

While our presentation of bi-Heyting algebras in
Definition
4.2
is not equational,
such a definition can be given
[
47
, 1.1]
,
showing that bi-Heyting algebras form a variety.
Thus, Theorem
2.14
allows us to conclude the following.

Corollary 4.16
.

The equivalent algebraic semantics of
\mathsf{sBIL}
are bi-Heyting algebras.

4.3
\mathsf{wBIL}
is equivalential

Now that we have situated
\mathsf{sBIL}
in the Leibniz hierarchy, we investigate

\mathsf{wBIL}
in the same way.
While we find that
\mathsf{wBIL}
is not algebraizable, we show that it is equivalential but not finitely so.
In fact, the relationship between
\mathsf{wBIL}
and bi-Heyting algebras
is so weak that we find it to not even have an algebraic semantics over

\mathcal{BHA}
.

We first prove that
\mathsf{wBIL}
is not algebraizable.
To show this, we use the Isomorphism theorem (Theorem
2.21
),
for which we need a characterisation of the
\mathsf{wBIL}
-filters (
) over bi-Heyting algebras:
they are lattice filters.

Definition 4.17

(
)
.

For any bi-Heyting algebra
A
, a
lattice filter
is a subset
F\subseteq A
such that:

1.

\dot{\top}\in F
;

2.

If
a\in F
and
a\leq b
then
b\in F
;

3.

If
a,b\in F
then
a\aland b\in F
.

This is to be expected as, for Heyting algebras, intuitionistic
filters are the lattice filters
[
14
, Exercise 2.31]
.

Lemma 4.18
.

For any bi-Heyting algebra
A
and subset
F\subseteq A
,
the following conditions are equivalent:

1.

F
is a
\mathsf{wBIL}
-filter;

2.

F
is a lattice filter.

Proof.

We prove the equivalence by showing separately that each statement implies the other.

(
1\Rightarrow 2
)

Let us first show that if
F
is a
\mathsf{wBIL}
-filter
it is then a lattice filter (
).

We start by showing that
\dot{\top}\in F
.
As
F
is a
\mathsf{wBIL}
-filter and
{}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\top}
, we get that
\hat{v}(\top)\in F

for any valuation
\hat{v}
over
A
. But
\hat{v}(\top)=\dot{\top}
by definition,
so
\dot{\top}\in F
.

Next, we show that
F
is closed under
\leq
.
Let
a,b\in F
such that
b\geq a
.
Then,
a\ato b=\dot{\top}
, hence
a\ato b\in F
given the above.
Let
\hat{v}
be a valuation over
A
such that
\hat{v}(p)=a
and
\hat{v}(q)=b
.
As
{p,p\to q}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{q}
and
\hat{v}(p),\hat{v}(p)\ato\hat{v}(q)\in F
,
then by the fact that
F
is a
\mathsf{wBIL}
-filter we get
\hat{v}(q)\in F
and hence
b\in F
.

Finally, we show that
F
is closed under (finite) meets.
Let
a,b\in F
, and let
\hat{v}
be a valuation over
A
such that
\hat{v}(p)=a
and
\hat{v}(q)=b
.
Then, as
{p,q}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{p\land q}
and
\hat{v}(p),\hat{v}(q)\in F
,
we get
\hat{v}(p)\aland\hat{v}(q)=\hat{v}(p\land q)\in F
as it is a
\mathsf{wBIL}
-filter.
Consequently,
a\aland b\in F
.

We conclude that
F
is a lattice filter.

(
2\Rightarrow 1
)

We now show that if
F
be a lattice filter it is then a

\mathsf{wBIL}
-filter (
).
We need to show that
F
is closed under consecutions: if
{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\varphi}

then for any valuation
v
over
A
, if
\hat{v}(\Gamma)\subseteq F
then

\hat{v}(\varphi)\in F
.
Assume that
{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\varphi}
. We prove our goal by induction on the derivation of
{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\varphi}
.
In each case, we let
v
be a valuation such that
\hat{v}(\Gamma)\subseteq F
and we show
\hat{v}(\varphi)\in F
.

The case for the rule (
El
) is straightforward, and the one for (
Ax
) can be treated as in
Proposition
4.8
, where it is shown that
\hat{v}(\psi)=\dot{\top}
for every
axiom
\psi
, as
\dot{\top}\in F
given that
F
is a lattice filter.

For the case of (
MP
), we get
\hat{v}(\psi)\in F
and
\hat{v}(\psi\to\theta)\in F

by induction hypothesis.
By definition, we get
\hat{v}(\psi)\ato\hat{v}(\theta)\in F
.
So,
\hat{v}(\psi)\aland(\hat{v}(\psi)\ato h(\theta))\in F
as
F
is a lattice filter.
As
\hat{v}(\psi)\aland(\hat{v}(\psi)\ato\hat{v}(\theta))\leq\hat{v}(\theta)
,
we get
\hat{v}(\theta)\in F
, solving the case for (
MP
).

Let us now show the case for (
wDN
).
It suffices to show that
\aneg\awneg\hat{v}(\psi)=\dot{\top}
,
as
\dot{\top}\in F
given that
F
is a lattice filter.
We prove this equality via the antisymmetry of
\leq
, and as

\dot{\top}
is the top element we are left to prove that

\dot{\top}\leq\aneg\awneg\hat{v}(\psi)
.
Using (ResI) we reduce this to
\dot{\top}\aland\awneg\hat{v}(\psi)\leq\abot
,
which is equivalent to
\awneg\hat{v}(\psi)\leq\abot
.
In turn, using (ResE) we further reduce our goal to
\dot{\top}\leq\hat{v}(\psi)\alor\abot
,
equivalent to
\dot{\top}\leq\hat{v}(\psi)
.
This result can be obtained by the fact that
\mathsf{sBIL}
is an extension of
\mathsf{wBIL}
(
),
giving us
{}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\psi}
which entails
\hat{v}(\psi)=\dot{\top}
by soundness of
\mathsf{sBIL}

(Proposition
4.8
).
Therefore,
F
is a
\mathsf{wBIL}
filter. ∎

We are now able to prove that
\mathsf{wBIL}
is not algebraizable, inspiring ourselves from
[
14
, Example 3.61]
.

Theorem 4.19
.

Weak bi-intuitionistic logic is not algebraizable.
13
13

13

The proof of this theorem has not been fully formalised,
as it crucially relies on the Isomorphism theorem, which we have not formalised due to its generality.
Still, the counterexample in the proof, i.e. the three elements chain, and its properties
are all formalised.

Proof.

Assume for a contradiction that
\mathsf{wBIL}
is algebraizable.
Then by the Isomorphism Theorem (Theorem
2.21
) and
Lemma
4.18

there exists an isomorphism between lattice filters of any bi-Heyting algebra
A
and
relative congruences
Con_{\mathcal{K}}(A)
. To show this is not true we give a
counter-example to this isomorphism.

We take
c_{3}=\{0,1/2,1\}
, the three element chain, as our needed
counter-example.

0
1/2
1

We define
a\ato b=1
if
a\leq b
and
a\ato b=b
otherwise.
Additionally, we define
a\aexcl b=0
if
a\leq b
and
a\aexcl b=a
otherwise.
It is known that this definition of
\ato
forms a Heyting algebra, so let us show
that our
\aexcl
makes it into a bi-Heyting algebra (
).

Assume that
a\aexcl b\leq c
.
If it is the case that
a\leq b
, then
a\leq b\alor c
is easily obtained.
Instead, if
a\not\leq b
then we have
a=a\aexcl b\leq c\leq b\alor c
.
In the other direction, if
a\leq b\alor c
, and
a\leq b
,
then
a\aexcl b=0\leq c
.
In the case where
a\not\leq b
, we get
a\aexcl b=a
.
Therefore it is sufficient to show
a\leq c
.
Given that
a\not\leq b
and
a\leq b\alor c
,
we can easily obtain this result.
Consequently,
c_{3}
is a bi-Heyting algebra.

Our next task consists in showing that
c_{3}
is simple, i.e. it has only two congruences (
).

Let
C
be any congruence on
c_{3}
.
Either
C=\Delta=\{(a,a)\mid a\in c_{3}\}
or
C\neq\Delta
.
If
C\neq\Delta
, there exist
a,b\in c_{3}
such that
(a,b)\in C
and
a\neq b
.
There are three cases for the given
a
and
b
:

1.

If
(0,1)\in C
, then
(0\aland 1/2,1\aland 1/2)=(0,1/2)\in C
and
so
C
is the trivial congruence.

2.

If
(0,1/2)\in C
, then
(0\ato 0,1/2\ato 0)=(1,1/2)
and so
C

is the trivial congruence.

3.

If
(1/2,1)\in C
, then
(1\aexcl 1/2,1\aexcl 1)=(1,0)\in C
and so

C
is the trivial congruence.

So, we can see that if
C\neq\Delta
, then
C
is the trivial congruence.
As a consequence, the cardinality of the relative congruences of
A
must be less
then or equal to
2
.

As all three distinct upsets on
c_{3}
are lattice filters (
,
,
), this means there cannot be an
isomorphism between the relative congruences of
A
and the
\mathsf{wBIL}
-filters
of
c_{3}
, therefore
\mathsf{wBIL}
is not algebraizable.
∎

We now show that
\mathsf{wBIL}
is equivalential with equivalence formulas

\Delta(x,y)=\{(\neg{\sim})^{n}(x\leftrightarrow y)\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.

Theorem 4.20
.

\mathsf{wBIL}
is equivalential with equivalence formulas
\{(\neg{\sim})^{n}(x\leftrightarrow y)\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.

Proof.

To prove
\mathsf{wBIL}
is equivalential we need to prove that
\Delta(x,y)
satisfies
the three properties (R), (MP’) and (Re) from Definition
2.18
.

First, we prove (R) (
). Note that
{}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{x\leftrightarrow y}
.
Thus, by repeated applications of (
wDN
), we easily obtain

{}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{(\neg{\sim})^{n}(x\leftrightarrow y)}
for all
n
, hence
\vdash_{w}\Delta(x,x)
.

Second, we focus on (MP’) (
).
Note that
{x,x\to y}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{y}
, which straightforwardly gives us
{x,x\leftrightarrow y}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{y}
.
Thus, by monotonicity we get
{x,\Delta(x,y)}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{y}
.

Third and last, we prove (Re) for every operator in
\mathsf{wBIL}
.
For the intuitionistic part we only show the cases of
\top
and
\to
,

,
,
).

Hence we can see
\mathsf{wBIL}
is an equivalential logic.
∎

The attentive reader may have noted that our set of equivalence formulas
\Delta(x,y)
is
not finite. One could therefore wonder whether there is a finite set of equivalence formulas
with which
\mathsf{wBIL}
can be shown to be equivalential, thereby making it
finitely

equivalential. In the remaining of this subsection, we provide a negative answer to this question.

To prove this, we make use of the following proposition as given in

[
14
, Proposition 6.65.5 and Proposition 6.65.6]
stating that
if a logic is finitely equivalential, then any set of equivalence formulas
for it can be finitarised.

Proposition 4.21
.

Let
\mathsf{L}
be some finite, finitary
equivalential logic with congruence formulas
\Delta(x,y)
. There exists a finite subset

\Delta^{\prime}(x,y)\subseteq\Delta(x,y)
such that
\Delta^{\prime}(x,y)\dashv\vdash\Delta(x,y)
and

\Delta^{\prime}(x,y)
is a set of congruence formulas for
\mathsf{L}
.

We now prove that
\mathsf{wBIL}
is not finitely equivalential. Our proof
essentially show that
no finitarisation of
\Delta(x,y)
can serve as set of equivalence
formulas for
\mathsf{wBIL}
.

Theorem 4.22

(
)
.

\mathsf{wBIL}
is not finitely equivalential.

Proof.

For a contradiction assume that
\mathsf{wBIL}
is finitely equivalential.
By Proposition
4.21
there exists a finite subset
\Delta^{\prime}(x,y)
of
\Delta(x,y)
,
which is a set of congruence formulas for
\mathsf{wBIL}
.
As
\Delta^{\prime}(x,y)
is finite, let
n
be the largest
n
such that
(\neg{\sim})^{n}(x\leftrightarrow y)\in\Delta^{\prime}(x,y)
.
Next, we show that the property (Re) fails for
\Delta^{\prime}(x,y)
, therefore showing that it does not constitute
a set of congruence formulas.

(
).
\scriptstyle 0

.

{\Delta^{\prime}(x_{1},x_{2}),\Delta^{\prime}(y_{1},y_{2})}\not\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\Delta^{\prime}(x_{1}\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}y_{2})}
by soundness.

This contradicts our initial assumption on the existence of a finite set of
congruence formulas.
∎

4.4
wBIL has no algebraic semantics over bi-Heyting algebras

We have shown that
\mathsf{wBIL}
does not have a strong relationship to bi-Heyting
algebras in the sense of algebraizability, but there is an obvious further
question: does it have any relationship at all? The answer, from an abstract
algebraic logic perspective, is no. There is no algebraic semantics that can be
constructed on bi-Heyting algebras for
\mathsf{wBIL}
.

Our proof of this statement makes a detour through the Kripke semantics
14
14

14

The idea on which this proof is based was discussed in private communications between
Shillito and Moraschini, which later influenced
[
36
, Corollary 9.7]
.
,
via the construction of a model consisting of a certain arrangement of
2n

copies of another given model
\mathfrak{M}
.

Definition 4.23

(
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}=(X,\leq,I)
be a Kripke model.
For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
we define
\mathfrak{M}_{k}=(X_{k},\leq_{k},I_{k})
to be a copy of
\mathfrak{M}

annotated by
k
, i.e. where
X_{k}=\{(x,k)\mid x\in X\}
,
(x,k)\leq_{k}(y,k)
if and only if
x\leq y
,
and
I_{k}(p)=\{(x,k)\mid x\in I(p)\}
.
Now, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
w\in X
, we define

\mathfrak{M}^{n}_{w}=(\{c\}\cup\bigcup\limits_{0\leq k\leq 2n}X_{k},\leq^{\prime},I^{\prime})
such that:

•

\leq^{\prime}
is the least preorder such that
\leq^{\prime}\supseteq\bigcup\limits_{0\leq i\leq n}\leq_{i}
and
(w,0)\leq^{\prime}(w,1)\geq^{\prime}...\leq^{\prime}(w,2n-1)\geq^{\prime}(w,2n)\leq^{\prime}c
;

•

I^{\prime}(p)=\{c\}\cup\bigcup_{0\leq k\leq 2n}I_{k}(p)
for any
p\in\Prop
.

We can depict the
model
\mathfrak{M}^{n}_{w}
constructed in the definition above as follows.
15
15

15

In the formalisation we used yet another copy
\mathfrak{M}_{n+1}

of
\mathfrak{M}
instead of
c
. The only difference in this last copy is that we
modify the interpretation
I_{n+1}
to make all propositional variables
everywhere in
X_{n+1}
.
\scriptstyle(w,0)
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
\scriptstyle(w,1)
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
\cdots
\scriptstyle(w,2n-1)
\mathfrak{M}_{2n-1}
\scriptstyle(w,2n)
\mathfrak{M}_{2n}
c

The model is constructed as
n
zig-zags
\lessgtr
of copies of
\mathfrak{M}
.
It is built so that
w
in
\mathfrak{M}
and
(w,0)
in
\mathfrak{M}^{n}_{w}

are
2n
-bisimilar (Definition
3.8
) and therefore force the same formulas
of bi-depth
2n
(Lemma
3.9
).
Notably,
w
and
(w,0)
force the same formulas of the shape
(\neg{\sim})^{n}\varphi

where
d(\varphi)=0
.
However, these two points are crucially not
2n+1
bisimilar,
as formulas of bi-depth
2n+1
can inspect
c
which has no
counterpart in
\mathfrak{M}
.
We support our claim of
2n
-bisimilarity below.

Lemma 4.24

(
)
.

For any model
\mathfrak{M}
and world
w\in\mathfrak{M}
, we have
\mathfrak{M},w\rightleftharpoons_{2n}\mathfrak{M}^{n}_{w},(w,0)
.

Proof.

We only provide an intuitive argument, and refer to our formalisation for a more detailed proof.
In essence, moves along
\leq^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{M}^{n}_{w}
can always be mimicked
in
\mathfrak{M}

as long as we have not moved to
c
.
The reason for this is that
c
has not corresponding world in
\mathfrak{M}
,
because of how valuation is defined on it.
Therefore, we get that
w
and
(w,0)
are
k
-bisimilar as long as
there is not path allowing us to move from
(w,0)
to
c
in
k
steps.
This is why we can prove
\mathfrak{M},w\rightleftharpoons_{2n}\mathfrak{M}^{n}_{w},(w,0)
:
the shortest path from
(w,0)
to
c
is via all
(w,i)
for
1\leq i\leq 2n
,
which is of length
2n+1
,
hence all paths of length smaller than
2n
are safe.
∎

Our proof that
\mathsf{wBIL}
has no algebraic semantics over bi-Heyting algebras heavily
relies on the construction outlined above, but also on two additional lemmas.

The first lemma is of a general nature, and is given in
[
4
, Theorem 2.16]
with a more
modern treatment in
[
36
, Proposition 3.3]
. We did not formalise it in our
work, but instead relied on one of its instances within our proof.

Lemma 4.25
.

If a logic
\mathsf{L}
has a
\tau(x)
-algebraic semantics, then for
all
\epsilon(x)=\delta(x)\in\tau(x)
and
\varphi(q,\overline{p})\in Form
,

x,\varphi(\epsilon(x),\overline{p})\dashv\vdash\varphi(\delta(x),\overline{p}),x.

For the second lemma, we take inspiration from Moraschini
[
36
, Proposition 9.6]
.

Lemma 4.26

(
)
.

For any
\varphi,\psi\in Form_{BI}
,

\mathcal{BHA}\vDash\varphi=\psi\quad\text{iff}\quad\varphi\dashv\vdash_{w}\psi.

Proof.

First, assume that
\varphi\dashv\vdash_{w}\psi
.
By the deduction theorem we get
{}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\varphi\to\psi}
and
{}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\psi\to\varphi}
,
which imply
{}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi\to\psi}
and
{}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\psi\to\varphi}
as
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathsf{sBIL}

agree on theorems (
).
By Theorem
4.15
, for every bi-heyting
algebra
A
and valuation
v
over
A
,

\hat{v}(\varphi\to\psi)=\dot{\top}
and
\hat{v}(\psi\to\varphi)=\dot{\top}
,
hence
\hat{v}(\varphi)\leq\hat{v}(\psi)
and
\hat{v}(\psi)\leq\hat{v}(\varphi)
.
The last two statements imply
\hat{v}(\varphi)=\hat{v}(\psi)
via antisymmetry of
\leq
.
As
A
and
v
are arbitrary, we proved
\mathcal{BHA}\vDash\varphi=\psi
.

One can prove the other direction by following the steps above, which are for most
them equivalences except for Theorem
4.15
which needs to be
replaced by Proposition
4.8
.
∎

We now have all the ingredients to prove that there is
no algebraic semantics on
\mathcal{BHA}
for
\mathsf{wBIL}
.
While the following proof is heavily inspired by Moraschini
[
36
, Proposition 9.6]
,
our case required a serious alteration of the model construction used in his proof.
16
16

16

The model construction in Moraschini’s proof consists in the addition of
a single extra world situated below a certain point in the initial model.
For our case we needed the more complex construction given in
Definition
4.23
.

Theorem 4.27

(
)
.

There is no
\tau
-algebraic semantics with respect to
\mathcal{BHA}
for which
\mathsf{wBIL}
is complete.

Proof.

Suppose, for a contradiction, that
\mathsf{wBIL}
is complete with respect to a
\tau
-algebraic semantics with respect to
\mathcal{BHA}
.

We first establish that there must be an equation
\epsilon(x)=\delta(x)\in\tau

such that
\mathcal{BHA}\not\vDash\epsilon(x)=\delta(x)
(
).
If this is not the case, then we would have
{}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{x}
by
the Definition
2.10
of
\tau
-algebraic semantics and the
assumption that
\mathsf{wBIL}
is complete for it.
By structurality of
\mathsf{wBIL}
, this implies that
{}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\bot}
.
But this is a contradiction, as
\mathsf{wBIL}
can be proved consistent
via soundness with respect to the Kripke semantics (Theorem
3.10
).

By Lemma
4.26
we can assume, without loss of generality,
that
{\epsilon(x)}\not\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\delta(x)}
.
Thus there exists a model
\mathfrak{M}=(W,\leq,I)
and a world
w\in W

such that
\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\epsilon(x)
and
\mathfrak{M},w\not\Vdash\delta(x)
.

Now, we exploit the model
\mathfrak{M}^{n}_{w}
generated from
\mathfrak{M}
and
w
following
Definition
4.23
, where
n=\max(d(\epsilon(x)),d(\delta(x)))
.
By Lemma
4.24
and Lemma
3.9
, we get

\mathfrak{M}^{n}_{w},(w,0)\Vdash\epsilon(x)
and
\mathfrak{M}^{n}_{w},(w,0)\not\Vdash\delta(x)
.
Given the zig-zag structure of
\mathfrak{M}^{n}_{w}
, we can use Lemma
3.6

to show that
\mathfrak{M}^{n}_{w},c\not\Vdash(\neg{\sim})^{n+1}(\epsilon(x)\to\delta(x))
,
as
c
can reach out to
(w,0)
in
n+1
steps of
\lessgtr
,
and
(w,0)
witnesses the failure of the implication
\epsilon(x)\to\delta(x)
.
Additionally, we get that
\mathfrak{M}^{n}_{w},c\Vdash x
by definition of the valuation in
c
.
Consequently, we have a proof of
x\not\mathrel{\kern 0.43057pt\text{\hbox to7.06pt{\vbox to7.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.10971pt\lower-3.76735pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.92 L 0 4.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.55 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 -1.95 -1.67 -3.77 -0.08 -4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 1.95 -1.67 3.77 -0.08 4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}${}_{g}$}}\kern 0.43057pt(\neg{\sim})^{n+1}(\epsilon(x)\to\delta(x))
which
gives us
{x}\not\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{(\neg{\sim})^{n}(\epsilon(x)\to\delta(x))}
.
As we additionally have that
{}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{(\neg{\sim})^{n+1}(\delta(x)\to\delta(x))}

via
n+1
applications of the rule (
wDN
), we can infer

{x,(\neg{\sim})^{n+1}(\delta(x)\to\delta(x))}\not\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{(\neg{\sim})^{n}(\epsilon(x)\to\delta(x))}
.
But this is a contradiction, as Lemma
4.25
informs us that this statement should hold
if
\tau
really is a
\tau
-algebraic semantics for
\mathsf{wBIL}
.

As our assumption on the existence of a
\tau
-algebraic semantics for which
\mathsf{wBIL}
is complete
led to a contradiction, we know that such a
\tau
cannot exist.
∎

5
Leveraging bridge theorems

In the previous section, we situated the logics
\mathsf{sBIL}
and
\mathsf{wBIL}
in the Leibniz hierarchy,
by showing that they were respectively finitely algebraizable and equivalential.
We can now harvest the fruits of our hard labour: we demonstrate the
use of the AAL approach by exploiting the so-called
bridge theorems
,
which equate properties of logics with properties of algebras.

While
\mathsf{wBIL}
does not have a strong link with its natural class of algebras
\mathcal{BHA}
,
the algebraizability of
\mathsf{sBIL}
with respect to
\mathcal{BHA}
shows that the connection
existing between the logic and the algebras is extremely tight.
We use this tight connection and bridge theorems for algebraizable logics
to show properties of both
\mathsf{sBIL}
and
\mathcal{BHA}
.

5.1
Craig interpolation and amalgamation

The first bridge theorem we inspect connects
Craig interpolation

to
amalgamation
. We start by defining the notion of Craig interpolation
following Czelakowski and Pigozzi
[
11
, Definition 3.1]
.
17
17

17

Arguably this is not Craig interpolation in the usual sense.
Traditionally, Craig Interpolation is expressed as the existence of an
interpolant
\chi(\overline{q})
of a provable implication

\vdash\varphi(\overline{p},\overline{q})\to\psi(\overline{q},\overline{r})
,
i.e. such that
\vdash\varphi(\overline{p},\overline{q})\to\chi(\overline{q})

and
\vdash\chi(\overline{q})\to\psi(\overline{q},\overline{r})
.
Still, Definition
5.1
can be seen as a language-agnostic
abstraction of the usual meaning of Craig Interpolation.

Definition 5.1

(Craig Interpolation)
.

A logic
\mathsf{L}
is said to have
Craig interpolation
if for

\Gamma(\overline{p},\overline{q})\vdash\varphi(\overline{q},\overline{r})
then
there exists a
\Gamma^{\prime}(\overline{q})
such that
\Gamma(\overline{p},\overline{q})\vdash\Gamma^{\prime}(\overline{q})
and

\Gamma^{\prime}(\overline{q})\vdash\varphi(\overline{q},\overline{r})
.

Next, we define the algebraic property called
amalgamation

[
11
, Definition
5.2]
.

Definition 5.2

(Amalgamation)
.

A variety
\mathcal{K}
has the amalgamation property if, for any given pair of
injective homomorphisms
f:C\to A
and
g:C\to B
with
A,B,C\in\mathcal{K}
there
exists a
D\in\mathcal{K}
and injective homomorphisms
h:A\to D
and
k:B\to D

such that
h\circ f=k\circ g
.

The bridge theorem connecting these two notions can be found in the same work
of Czelakowski and Pigozzi
[
11
, Theorem 3.6,Corollary 5.28]
.

Theorem 5.3
.

If a logic
\mathsf{L}
is algebraizable with equivalent algebraic semantics
\mathcal{K}
, then the following holds.

\mathsf{L}
has Craig interpolation

iff

\mathcal{K}
has amalgamation.

Using this bridge, we can prove the following result about
\mathsf{sBIL}
and
\mathcal{BHA}
.

Corollary 5.4
.

\mathsf{sBIL}
has Craig interpolation and
\mathcal{BHA}
has amalgamation.

Proof.

It was proved by Galli, Reyes and Sagastume
[
16
]
that
\mathcal{BHA}
has strong amalgamation,
a property which implies amalgamation. So, we know that
\mathcal{BHA}
has
amalgamation, and using Theorem
5.3
we can conclude that

\mathsf{sBIL}
has Craig interpolation.

An alternative, and inverse, proof starts from the fact that
\mathsf{wBIL}
has Craig interpolation
in the usual form
[
30
,
33
]
.
As
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathsf{sBIL}
coincide on their theorems, which is where the usual notion of
Craig interpolation is set, we therefore get that
\mathsf{sBIL}
has the usual Craig interpolation.
Now, we can show that Craig interpolation in the sense of Definition
5.1

can be obtained using the usual sense.
Assume that
{\Gamma(\overline{p},\overline{q})}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi(\overline{q},\overline{r})}
.
Then, as
\mathsf{sBIL}
is a finitary logic, there must be a finite subset

\Delta(\overline{p},\overline{q})\subseteq\Gamma(\overline{p},\overline{q})
such that

{\Delta(\overline{p},\overline{q})}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi(\overline{q},\overline{r})}
.
Therefore, we can form the conjunction of all its elements and get

{\bigwedge\Delta(\overline{p},\overline{q})}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi(\overline{q},\overline{r})}
.
By Lemma
3.2
we get that there is a
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that

{}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{(\neg{\sim})^{n}(\bigwedge\Delta(\overline{p},\overline{q}))\to\varphi(\overline{q},\overline{r})}
.
Therefore we can use the usual Craig interpolation to get a
\chi(\overline{q})
such that

{}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{(\neg{\sim})^{n}(\bigwedge\Delta(\overline{p},\overline{q}))\to\chi(\overline{q})}
and

{}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\chi(\overline{q})\to\varphi(\overline{q},\overline{r})}
.
These last statements straightforwardly lead to

{\Delta(\overline{p},\overline{q})}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\chi(\overline{q})}
and

{\chi(\overline{q})}\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{\varphi(\overline{q},\overline{r})}
.
So,
\chi(\overline{q})
is the
\Gamma^{\prime}(\overline{q})
we were looking for.
As Craig interpolation in the sense of Definition
5.1
holds for
\mathsf{sBIL}
,
we therefore get that
\mathcal{BHA}
has amalgamation via Theorem
5.3
.
∎

5.2
Right deductive uniform interpolation and coherence

The second bridge theorem we consider relates
right deductive uniform interpolation

with
coherence
, which we both define next.

Definition 5.5

(Right deductive uniform interpolation)
.

A logic
\mathsf{L}
is said to have
right deductive uniform interpolation
if for
any formula
\varphi
and
p\in\Prop
, there is a formula
\chi
such that
(1)
p
does not appear in
\chi
,
(2)
\varphi\vdash\chi
, and
(3) for any formula
\psi
not containing
p
such that

\varphi\vdash\psi
we have
\chi\vdash\psi
.

Definition 5.6

(Coherence)
.

A variety
\mathcal{V}
is
coherent

if every finitely generated subalgebra of a finitely presented member
of
\mathcal{V}
is finitely presented.

The bridge theorem we are interested in connects the both notions in the following way
[
29
, Proposition 2.4]
.

Theorem 5.7
.

If a logic
\mathsf{L}
is algebraizable with equivalent algebraic semantics
\mathcal{K}
, then the following holds.

\mathsf{L}
satisfies right deductive uniform interpolation

iff

\mathcal{K}
is coherent and admits deductive interpolation.

While there are some elements we left undefined in Definition
5.6

and Theorem
5.7
18
18

18

We refer the interested reader to
[
57
,
29
]
.
, we want the reader to take away the following: if the class of algebras corresponding
to an algebraizable logic is not coherent, then the logic fails right deductive uniform
interpolation.
We therefore obtain the following.

Corollary 5.8
.

The logic
\mathsf{sBIL}
fails right deductive uniform interpolation.

Proof.

Given Theorem
5.7
, it suffices to show that
\mathcal{BHA}
is not coherent.
The latter was proved by Kowalski and Metcalfe
[
29
, Corollary 5.8]
.
So,
\mathsf{sBIL}
fails right deductive uniform interpolation.
∎

5.3
Deduction-detachment theorem and equationally definable principal congruences

Finally, we consider a bridge theorem bringing together the

deduction-detachment theorem
and the property of having

equationally definable principal congruences
.

The notion of deduction-detachment theorem we consider
here is a generalised language agnostic version of the traditional notion.
The following definition can be found in Font’s textbook
[
14
, Definition 3.76]
.

Definition 5.9

(Deduction-detachment theorem)
.

A set of formulas
I(p,q)\subseteq Form
in at most two variables is a

Deduction-Detachment (DD) set
for a logic
\vdash
when, for all

\Gamma\cup\{\varphi,\psi\}\subseteq Form
,

\Gamma,\varphi\vdash\psi\quad\text{ iff }\quad\Gamma\vdash I(\varphi,\psi).

A logic
satisfies the Deduction-Detachment Theorem
(DDT) when it has a
DD set.

To define the algebraic property corresponding to the DDT for
some algebraizable logics, we make use of a specific type of
congruence on an algebra
A
generated from a pair

(a,b)
of elements of
A
:
we define
\Omega^{A}(a,b)
to be the smallest congruence
on
A
that identifies
a
and
b
.

Definition 5.10

(Equationally definable principal congruences)
.

A variety
\mathcal{K}
has
equationally definable principal congruences
(EDPC),
when there is a finite non-empty set of equations in four variables

\{\eta_{i}(x_{0},x_{1},y_{0},y_{1})=\zeta_{i}(x_{0},x_{1},y_{0},y_{1})\mid i=1,...,m\}
such that for all
A\in\mathcal{K}
and

a,b,c,d\in A
,

(c,d)\in\Omega_{A}(a,b)\quad\text{iff}\quad\eta_{i}(a,b,c,d)=\zeta_{i}(a,b,c,d)\text{ for all }i.

Standard examples of varieties having EDPC are Boolean and Heyting algebras, as
they satisfy the following equivalence:

(c,d)\in\Omega_{A}(a,b)
if and only if

((a\ato b)\aland(b\ato a))\aland c=((a\ato b)\aland(b\ato a))\aland d
.

We will now give the bridge theorem connecting the DDT and EDPC.
This is a famous result originally proven by Blok and Pigozzi
[
2
]
,
with a textbook account given by Font
[
14
, Corollary 3.86]
.

Theorem 5.11
.

If a finitary logic
\mathsf{L}
is finitely algebraizable with equivalent
algebraic semantics
\mathcal{K}
, then the following holds.

\mathsf{L}
satisfies DDT

iff

\mathcal{K}
has EDPC.

Leveraging the theorem above, we prove that
\mathsf{sBIL}
does not have the DDT
by showing that
\mathcal{BHA}
does not have EDPC.
To do so we make use of the following characterisation of varieties of
bi-Heyting algebras that have the EDPC
[
54
, Corollary 3.7]
.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a variety of bi-Heyting algebras, then
\mathcal{K}
has the EDPC if and only
if there exists an
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for every
A\in\mathcal{K}
and
a\in A
we have

(\aDN)^{n+1}(a)=(\aDN)^{n}(a)
.

Given this we can now see that
\mathsf{sBIL}
does not satisfy DDT.

Theorem 5.13
.

The logic
\mathsf{sBIL}
does not satisfy DDT.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.12
and Theorem
5.11
it
suffices to construct a bi-Heyting algebra
A
with an
a\in A
which satisfy

(\neg{\sim})^{n+1}(a)\neq(\neg{\sim})^{n}(a)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Consider the Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra
\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\emptyset}
.
Given that
{}\not\vdash_{\mathsf{s}}{(\neg{\sim})^{n}p\to(\neg{\sim})^{n+1}p}
for every
n
,
we can conclude that
\llbracket(\neg{\sim})^{n}p\rrbracket\neq\llbracket(\neg{\sim})^{n+1}p\rrbracket
,
i.e.
(\aDN)^{n}\llbracket p\rrbracket\neq(\aDN)^{n+1}\llbracket p\rrbracket
.
This shows that
\mathcal{BHA}
does not have EDPC,
hence
\mathsf{sBIL}
does not satisfy DDT.
∎

It was already known that
\mathsf{sBIL}
only satisfies a modified version of
the traditional deduction-detachment theorem, displayed in Lemma
3.2
.
Still, the result above is new as it generalises this failure of the
traditional deduction-detachment theorem, which is nothing but
an instance of the failure of the DDT for
\mathsf{sBIL}
.

6
Stepping aside AAL: logics preserving (degrees of) truth

The AAL analysis is clear:
\mathsf{sBIL}
is tightly connected to
\mathcal{BHA}
, as it is algebraizable,
but
\mathsf{wBIL}
not at all, as it has no
\tau
-algebraic semantics over it.
One may be finding this state of affairs too harsh, as
\mathsf{wBIL}
and
\mathcal{BHA}
can surely
be connected in some way.

In this section, we briefly provide another framework which gives an
algebraic semantic for both
\mathsf{sBIL}
and
\mathsf{wBIL}
over
\mathcal{BHA}
.
More precisely, in this context

\mathsf{sBIL}
is the logic
preserving truth
over
\mathcal{BHA}

while
\mathsf{wBIL}
is the logic
preserving degrees of truth
over
\mathcal{BHA}

[
38
]
.

Let us start with
\mathsf{sBIL}
.

Definition 6.1

(Logic preserving truth)
.

The logic preserving truth
\vdash_{pt}
on a class
\mathcal{K}
of algebras with a top element
\dot{\top}
19
19

19

We consider a restricted version of this notion which works for our case:
here we use
\dot{\top}
in our notion, but one can generalise it using arbitrary designated point.

is defined as

\Gamma\vdash_{pt}\varphi

:=

\forall A\in\mathcal{K}.\,\forall v.\,\;\text{if }(\forall\gamma\in\Gamma.\;\dot{\top}=\hat{v}(\gamma))\text{ then }\dot{\top}=\hat{v}(\varphi).

By looking at the definition above, one can directly see that the statement
that
\mathsf{sBIL}
is the logic preserving truth over
\mathcal{BHA}
is nothing but the combination
of the already established Proposition
4.8
and Proposition
4.12
.

Corollary 6.2
.

The logic
\mathsf{sBIL}
is the logic preserving truth over
\mathcal{BHA}
.

Now, we focus
\mathsf{wBIL}
. We can retrieve an algebraic semantics over
\mathcal{BHA}

for this logic via the notion of logic preserving degrees of truth.

Definition 6.3

(Logic preserving degrees of truth
)
.

The logic preserving degrees of truth
\vdash_{pdt}
on a class
\mathcal{K}
of algebras is defined as follows,
where
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq_{f}\Gamma
means
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
is finite.
20
20

20

Two remarks can be made about our definition.
First, as in Definition
6.1
we use a restricted definition of this notion which
assumes the definability of inequality in the algebras.
Second, the use of a finite
\Gamma^{\prime}
instead of
\Gamma
may look
unnatural, as it builds in the compactness of the logic.
We decided to use this definition for two reasons.
First, we took inspiration from Remark 2.4 of
[
37
]
which
claims that one can focus their attention to the finitary case.
Second, the use of an infinite
\Gamma
in this definition
would not allow our proof of Theorem
6.5
below
to go through, as we shall explain later.

\Gamma\vdash_{pdt}\varphi

:=

\exists\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq_{f}\Gamma.\,\forall A\in\mathcal{K}.\,\forall v.\,\forall a.\,\;\text{if }(\forall\gamma\in\Gamma^{\prime}.\;a\leq\hat{v}(\gamma))\text{ then }a\leq\hat{v}(\varphi)

To show that
\mathsf{wBIL}
is the logic preserving degrees of truth of
\mathcal{BHA}
,
we use a Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra
\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{w}}_{\emptyset}
for
\mathsf{wBIL}
built
as in Definition
4.10
(
).
Here, it is crucial that we use the empty set of formulas as context as
this restriction allows us to prove that
\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{w}}_{\emptyset}\in\mathcal{BHA}

when defining
\aexcl
in
\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{w}}_{\emptyset}
as the operator working on
representative of equivalence classes in
\llbracket Form\rrbracket^{\mathsf{w}}_{\emptyset}
.
As already noted, if we allowed non-empty contexts, then defining the
\aexcl
in this
way would fail to give us a bi-Heyting algebra.

As in
\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\Gamma}
we get that formulas in
\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{w}}_{\emptyset}
are
interpreted as their equivalence class.

Lemma 6.4

(
)
.

Let
v_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{}}}:Prop\to\llbracket Form\rrbracket^{\mathsf{w}}_{\emptyset}
such that
v_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{}}}(p)=\llbracket p\rrbracket
. We have that
\hat{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{}}}(\varphi)=\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one for Lemma
4.11
.
The only interesting point to note here is that while the latter
proof implicitly relied on monotonicity laws for
\mathrel{\hskip 0.43057pt\hbox to8.96pt{\vbox to4.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.71042pt\lower-2.0882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.6 0 L 8.94 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.04 0 L 10.96 2.86 L 11.38 2.49 L 9.23 0 L 11.38 -2.49 L 10.96 -2.86 L 8.04 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 0.43057pt}

in presence of a context
\Gamma
,
this proof relies on these laws in the
empty
context.
The former laws hold only in
\mathsf{sBIL}
, but the latter hold in both.
∎

Relying on the above, we straightforwardly prove
\mathsf{wBIL}
to be
the logic preserving degrees of truth of
\mathcal{BHA}
.

Theorem 6.5
.

The logic
\mathsf{wBIL}
is the logic preserving truth over
\mathcal{BHA}
.

Proof.

Assume
\Gamma\vdash_{pdt}\varphi
.
By definition, there is a finite
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma
such that

\forall A\in\mathcal{K}.\,\forall v.\,\forall a.\,\;\text{if }(\forall\gamma\in\Gamma^{\prime}.\;a\leq\hat{v}(\gamma))\text{ then }a\leq\hat{v}(\varphi).

Given that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is finite, we can create the conjunction of all its elements
\bigwedge\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Now, if in the statement above we set
\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{w}}_{\emptyset}
for
A
, and
v_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{}}}
from Lemma
6.4
for
v
,
as well as
\llbracket\bigwedge\Gamma^{\prime}\rrbracket
for
a
, we get the following.
21
21

21

The finiteness of
\Gamma^{\prime}
allows us to form the conjunction of all its elements,
which is a formula and therefore has a corresponding equivalence class in
\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{w}}_{\emptyset}
.
If instead we used an infinite
\Gamma
, we would not be able to perform this move, as we are not sure whether
the infinite conjunction
\bigwedge\Gamma
has a corresponding element (with adequate properties)
in
\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{w}}_{\emptyset}
.
A way to reconcile a version of Definition
6.3
where
\Gamma
is not reduced to a finite subset
with a proof of Theorem
6.5
could consist in considering the
canonical extension

of
\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{w}}_{\emptyset}
, which provides adequate infinite meets
[
26
,
18
,
19
,
20
,
17
]
.

\text{if }(\forall\gamma\in\Gamma^{\prime}.\;\llbracket\bigwedge\Gamma^{\prime}\rrbracket\leq\hat{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{}}}(\gamma))\text{ then }\llbracket\bigwedge\Gamma^{\prime}\rrbracket\leq\hat{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{}}}(\varphi)

By properties of meet, we can easily show that

\forall\gamma\in\Gamma^{\prime}.\;\llbracket\bigwedge\Gamma^{\prime}\rrbracket\leq\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket
,
which gives us the antecedent of the implication above as
\hat{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{}}}(\gamma)=\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket

via Lemma
6.4
.
Therefore, we get
\llbracket\bigwedge\Gamma^{\prime}\rrbracket\leq\hat{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{}}}(\varphi)
hence
\llbracket\bigwedge\Gamma^{\prime}\rrbracket\leq\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket

thanks to another application of Lemma
6.4
.
We easily obtain
\llbracket\top\rrbracket\leq\llbracket(\bigwedge\Gamma^{\prime})\to\varphi\rrbracket
via properties of bi-Heyting algebras
and the definition of
\mathbb{LT}^{\mathsf{w}}_{\emptyset}
.
Consequently, we have that
\llbracket\top\rrbracket
and
\llbracket(\bigwedge\Gamma^{\prime})\to\varphi\rrbracket
are the same equivalence
classes, which implies that
{}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\top\to((\bigwedge\Gamma^{\prime})\to\varphi)}
hence
{}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{(\bigwedge\Gamma^{\prime})\to\varphi}
.
Using the deduction-detachment theorem from Lemma
3.2
,
we get that
{\bigwedge\Gamma^{\prime}}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\varphi}
hence
{\Gamma^{\prime}}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\varphi}
.
A last application of monotonicity (Definition
2.9
) gives us our goal
{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\varphi}
.

The other direction, showing
\Gamma\vdash_{pdt}\varphi
from
{\Gamma}\vdash_{\mathsf{w}}{\varphi}
is a straightforward soundness argument.
∎

So, in this light
\mathsf{wBIL}
can be seen as having a semantics on the class of bi-Heyting algebras.
The downside, however, is that this type of semantics does not come equipped with the strength
of the AAL framework exhibited in Section
5
.

7
Conclusion

In this paper we laid formalised, hence solid, foundations to the algebraic treatment of bi-intuitionistic logics,
a field which had not been thoroughly revisited since the discoveries of mistakes in Rauszer’s works.

More precisely, we formalised in the interactive theorem prover Rocq results situating the logics
\mathsf{sBIL}

and
\mathsf{wBIL}
in the Leibniz hierarchy, by showing that
the former is implicative and finitely algebraizable over the class
\mathcal{BHA}
of bi-Heyting algebras,
while the latter is not algebraizable, has no algebraic semantic over
\mathcal{BHA}
, but is non-finitely equivalential.

We exploited the algebraizability of
\mathsf{sBIL}
to showcase the strength of the abstract algebraic logic framework:
we could show that
\mathsf{sBIL}
has Craig interpolation but lacks both right deductive uniform interpolation and
a deduction-detachment theorem, by using bridge theorems and known facts about
\mathcal{BHA}
,
i.e. that it has amalgamation but fails coherence and does not have equationally definable principal congruences.

Finally, we made a step aside the abstract algebraic logic framework to show that one
could still connect the logic
\mathsf{wBIL}
to
\mathcal{BHA}
by the former as the logic preserving
degrees of truth of the latter. In this context,
\mathsf{sBIL}
becomes the logic preserving truth of
\mathcal{BHA}
.
